CAT-VARC-P03-001 · Cities and space · passage Easy · 453 words · 4 questions

For much of the last century, the standard official response to an informal settlement was to remove it. Planners looked at a hillside of self-built houses, narrow lanes and improvised water connections and saw a problem of housing quality, to be solved by demolition and the construction of orderly blocks elsewhere, usually on cheaper land at the edge of the city. The logic seemed humane as well as tidy: people living without drains or secure walls would be given drains and secure walls. That the residents frequently resisted, and that many of the new blocks were half empty within a few years, was read as ingratitude, or as evidence that the poor did not know their own interests.

A different reading is now more common, and it is more persuasive. An informal settlement is not simply a stock of poor dwellings; it is a working arrangement of livelihoods, and its location is usually its chief asset. A family that sells cooked food near a bus depot, a tailor who takes in work from a nearby market, a mother whose children attend a school within walking distance: all of them chose the site because of what it is close to. Relocation to a distant estate improves the walls while destroying the arrangement. The household may gain a toilet and lose two hours a day to travel, or lose the income that made the new rent payable at all. Seen this way, the empty blocks were not a puzzle but a verdict.

Upgrading in place starts from this recognition. Instead of replacing the settlement, it adds what is missing: paved lanes, drainage, water and sewer lines, street lighting and, crucially, some form of secure tenure, so that residents can invest in their houses without fearing that the next official survey will mark them for demolition. Because the residents remain where they are, the networks that sustain them survive, and the public money spent goes into infrastructure rather than into reproducing, at great cost and in the wrong place, a community that already existed.

None of this makes upgrading a universal remedy. Some settlements stand on land that floods every monsoon or slides in heavy rain, and to improve them in place is to invest in a future disaster. Nor is upgrading automatically benign: lanes widened for drainage can require the demolition of some houses, and a programme that installs pipes but withholds tenure leaves residents improved and still insecure. The case for upgrading is therefore not that informal settlements are good as they are, a romantic view their residents rarely share. It is that the people who built them understood something about the city that the planners who wished to clear them did not.

CAT-VARC-P03-001-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)show that secure tenure matters more to the residents of informal settlements than drains, water supply or paved lanes.
  2. (B)argue that improving informal settlements where they stand generally serves residents better than relocating them, while noting where this approach falls short.
  3. (C)argue that informal settlements, having been built by their own residents, should be left as they are rather than altered by public authorities.
  4. (D)explain why planners in the last century preferred to build replacement housing on cheap land at the edge of the city.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 describes the old policy of clearance, paragraph 2 explains why it failed (location sustains livelihoods), paragraph 3 presents upgrading in place as the better alternative, and paragraph 4 notes its limits (hazardous land, demolitions, missing tenure). The correct answer captures both the thesis and the concession.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 lists tenure among the things upgrading adds and calls it crucial, but it never ranks tenure above infrastructure; the option invents a comparison.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 calls the view that settlements are 'good as they are' a romantic one 'their residents rarely share', and paragraph 3 endorses adding lanes, drains and tenure.

Why not (D): The location of the new blocks is a detail of paragraph 1; the passage as a whole is about why upgrading in place is the better response, so the option is too narrow.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that settlements should be left as they are because the author is sympathetic to residents, and missing the explicit rejection of the romantic view.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated plainly in paragraphs 2 and 3; the wrong options are a reversal, a detail and an invented ranking. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-001-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the relocation blocks were often half empty within a few years because
  1. (A)the land chosen for the estates was prone to flooding and landslides during the heavy rains of the monsoon.
  2. (B)the new blocks lacked the drains and secure walls that the residents had been promised before they agreed to move.
  3. (C)the residents were ungrateful and did not understand which kind of housing would serve their interests best.
  4. (D)moving to them cut households off from the work, schools and markets that had made their earlier location valuable.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 says families chose their sites 'because of what it is close to', and that relocation to a distant estate 'improves the walls while destroying the arrangement', costing travel time or income. The empty blocks were 'a verdict' on that loss.

Why not (A): Flooding and landslides are mentioned in paragraph 4 about some informal settlements, not about the relocation estates.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says relocation 'improves the walls'; the problem was what the move destroyed, not missing facilities in the blocks.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 reports this as how officials 'read' the empty blocks; paragraph 2 rejects that reading as less persuasive than the author's own.

Common misconception: Picking the official explanation in paragraph 1, which the author reports only to reject.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in paragraph 2; the main trap is a view the author reports but rejects. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-001-Q3 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
In paragraph 2, the statement that the empty blocks were 'not a puzzle but a verdict' means that the empty blocks were
  1. (A)a judgement on relocation that residents delivered by moving out, once the costs of distance are understood.
  2. (B)a court ruling that later stopped the authorities from clearing further settlements across the city.
  3. (C)a mystery that planners could not explain, even after they had studied the residents' reasons closely.
  4. (D)an official finding that the new housing had been badly designed and poorly built by the authorities.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The sentence follows the account of what relocation cost households. By leaving the blocks, residents in effect pronounced judgement on the policy; their behaviour is explained, not mysterious, once the importance of location is seen.

Why not (B): The passage mentions no legal proceedings; 'verdict' is used figuratively for the residents' response.

Why not (C): This is the 'puzzle' reading the sentence explicitly denies; once location is understood, the empty blocks need no special explanation.

Why not (D): No official finding is mentioned, and the passage says relocation improved the walls; the verdict was passed by residents, not by officials.

Common misconception: Taking 'verdict' literally as a legal or official decision.

Difficulty: Figurative phrase explained by the sentences just before it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-001-Q4 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely describe a programme that paves the lanes and lays sewers in a settlement but gives residents no protection against eviction as
  1. (A)a romantic attempt to keep the settlement exactly as it was, against the wishes of most of its residents.
  2. (B)a successful upgrade, because residents stay where they are and the networks that sustain them are preserved.
  3. (C)an incomplete improvement, because residents would remain unwilling to invest in houses they might still lose.
  4. (D)worse than relocation, because improving such a settlement amounts to investing in a future disaster.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says tenure is needed 'so that residents can invest in their houses without fearing' demolition, and paragraph 4 says a programme that 'installs pipes but withholds tenure leaves residents improved and still insecure'. The author would see such a programme as falling short.

Why not (A): Paving lanes and laying sewers changes the settlement; the romantic view in paragraph 4 is the belief that settlements are good unchanged.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 calls secure tenure crucial to upgrading, and paragraph 4 says a programme that 'installs pipes but withholds tenure' leaves residents 'still insecure', so the author would not call it a success.

Why not (D): The 'future disaster' remark in paragraph 4 concerns settlements on land that floods or slides, not settlements lacking tenure.

Common misconception: Choosing the 'successful upgrade' option because staying in place is the advantage the passage stresses, and overlooking the tenure condition.

Difficulty: Requires connecting the tenure clause in paragraph 3 with the caution in paragraph 4; one wrong option states a real advantage of upgrading. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-002 · Cities and space · passage Easy · 440 words · 4 questions

Few words in urban debate do as much damage as 'density', largely because it is used, often by the same speaker within a single argument, to mean two quite different things. In its technical sense density is a ratio: the number of people, or of dwellings, on a given area of land. In ordinary speech it usually means something closer to crowding, the experience of too many people in too little room: a family of six in a single room, a queue that spills into the road, a train in which nobody can turn round. The two can occur together, but they need not, and treating them as one has shaped a century of planning.

Consider two districts with the same number of residents per hectare. In the first, people live in tall apartment buildings with generous flats, set among parks and wide streets. In the second, the same population is packed into low houses, several households to a building, with lanes too narrow for a cart. On a map of density the districts are identical; to anyone who lives in them they could hardly be more different. Conversely, a scattered agricultural village, where extended families share a single dwelling, can be acutely crowded indoors while being nearly empty by the hectare. Density, in short, describes land; crowding describes the conditions of daily life.

The confusion has a history. The diseases that ravaged industrial cities in the nineteenth century were widely blamed on density, and reformers responded by trying to spread people out. Yet what made those cities so lethal was a combination of overcrowded rooms, contaminated water and absent sewers, and where water and drains were improved, death rates in dense districts fell even though the districts often remained dense. The reformers were right that something was wrong; they were imprecise about what it was, and the imprecision outlived them, surviving in zoning codes and building regulations long after the sanitary conditions that had justified it had disappeared.

This is not to say that density is costless. Many people living close together compete for parks, school places and quiet, and a district can feel oppressive even when no room in it is overcrowded. Perception counts, and planners who dismiss it will find their schemes resented. But the remedies differ. Crowding is relieved by more floor space per person and by services; the costs of density are managed by design, by open space and by the quality of the street. A policy that caps density in the name of relieving crowding may achieve neither, pushing people into fewer and more crowded rooms, or out to the edge of the city.

CAT-VARC-P03-002-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, density differs from crowding in that
  1. (A)density is a ratio of people or dwellings to land, while crowding concerns too many people for the room they have.
  2. (B)density is found mainly in large cities, while crowding is a problem that arises chiefly in villages.
  3. (C)density damages the health of residents, while crowding is merely uncomfortable for those who experience it.
  4. (D)density refers to how many people share a room, while crowding refers to how many live on a hectare.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 defines density technically as 'the number of people, or of dwellings, on a given area of land' and crowding as 'too many people in too little room'; paragraph 2 sums up: 'Density ... describes land; crowding describes the conditions of daily life.'

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 uses the village only to show that crowding can occur without density; it does not confine either condition to one kind of place.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the lethal conditions were overcrowded rooms and bad sanitation, and that death rates fell even where districts stayed dense, which is close to the opposite.

Why not (D): This reverses the definitions in paragraph 1, where density is people 'on a given area of land' and crowding is 'too many people in too little room'.

Common misconception: Reversing the two definitions under time pressure.

Difficulty: Both definitions are stated directly in paragraphs 1 and 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-002-Q2 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The comparison of two districts with the same number of residents per hectare (paragraph 2) serves mainly to
  1. (A)suggest that maps showing population per hectare are usually drawn from inaccurate or outdated figures.
  2. (B)show that areas equal in density can differ sharply in how crowded daily life within them is.
  3. (C)prove that tall apartment buildings are always better places to live than low houses on narrow lanes.
  4. (D)illustrate the method by which nineteenth-century reformers tried to spread the urban population out.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The districts share a density figure but one offers generous flats among parks and the other packs several households to a building. The paragraph concludes that 'density ... describes land; crowding describes the conditions of daily life'.

Why not (A): The passage treats the maps as accurate; the districts are identical 'On a map of density', and the point is that density measures land, not daily life.

Why not (C): The point is that density figures hide differences in living conditions, not that one building type is always superior; the word always goes beyond the example.

Why not (D): The reformers appear in paragraph 3; the two districts are a hypothetical comparison with no connection to their methods.

Common misconception: Reading the example as a recommendation of tower blocks.

Difficulty: The function is stated in the paragraph's closing sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-002-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The passage suggests that the nineteenth-century reformers mentioned in paragraph 3
  1. (A)deliberately exaggerated the dangers of density in order to win public support for large building programmes.
  2. (B)succeeded in lowering death rates in industrial cities chiefly by persuading residents to move to less dense districts.
  3. (C)were mistaken in thinking that anything about conditions in the industrial cities needed to be changed at all.
  4. (D)rightly sensed that the industrial cities were unhealthy but located the cause in density rather than in rooms and sanitation.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The reformers blamed density and tried to 'spread people out', but the lethal factors were 'overcrowded rooms, contaminated water and absent sewers', and death rates fell with better water and drains even where density stayed high. They were 'right that something was wrong' but 'imprecise about what it was'.

Why not (A): Nothing is said about the reformers' motives; the passage calls them 'imprecise', which implies an honest error, not deliberate exaggeration.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 credits improved water and drains with lowering death rates, and notes that districts 'often remained dense', so the fall was not due to dispersal.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says 'The reformers were right that something was wrong', so the author does not think their concern mistaken.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that credits dispersal with lower death rates, because reformers acted and death rates fell.

Difficulty: Requires combining the cause of mortality with the observation that density often stayed high; one wrong option confuses sequence with cause. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-002-Q4 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
A city has a district in which several families typically share one room. Which approach would the author most likely recommend?
  1. (A)Capping the number of residents per hectare and moving those above the limit to new settlements on the edge of the city.
  2. (B)Replacing every low house in the district with high towers, because building upward removes crowding wherever it is tried.
  3. (C)Increasing the floor space available to each household and supplying water and drains, without necessarily reducing residents per hectare.
  4. (D)Leaving the district as it is, since residents' sense of being crowded counts for less than the measured density of the land.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Several families to a room is crowding, not merely density. Paragraph 4 says 'Crowding is relieved by more floor space per person and by services', and paragraph 3 shows sanitation lowering death rates without lowering density.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 warns that capping density to relieve crowding 'may achieve neither', pushing people into more crowded rooms or out to the city's edge.

Why not (B): The towers in paragraph 2 relieve crowding because their flats are 'generous'; the author never says height by itself removes crowding, and the claim that it works wherever it is tried overreaches.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says 'Perception counts' and the district described here is crowded in the literal sense, so the author would not leave it alone.

Common misconception: Choosing the density cap because the district sounds 'dense', which is exactly the confusion the passage criticises.

Difficulty: Requires classifying the case as crowding and applying the matching remedy; the tower option borrows the paragraph 2 example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-003 · Cities and space · passage Medium · 412 words · 4 questions

In the last two decades the walkable neighbourhood has become one of the few planning ideals on which almost everyone claims to agree, and with agreement has come measurement. Composite indices now rate districts by the number of shops, schools and stops within a short radius, the density of street intersections and the share of homes near public transport. Estate agents quote such figures; municipal plans set targets for raising them. It would be churlish to deny the good these indices have done. They gave walking, long treated as what people did when they could not drive, a place in the arithmetic of planning, and they made visible how many districts had been designed as if nobody would ever arrive on foot.

Yet what the indices measure is proximity, and proximity is not the same as walkability. A street can lie within five minutes of a pharmacy, a school and a bus stop and still be miserable to walk along: a narrow pavement beside four lanes of fast traffic, no shade under a summer sun, the blank walls of parking structures on either side, crossings that demand a long detour. Two districts with identical scores can therefore offer entirely different experiences, and the index has no way of registering the difference, because it counts what lies at the end of the walk and ignores the walk itself.

This blindness is not accidental; it reflects an assumption that walking is chiefly a means of reaching places. For many people it is that, but it is also something more: the way a child learns a neighbourhood, the way an elderly resident keeps in contact with the world, the occasion for the chance encounters on which local life depends. These goods are produced en route, by the quality of the street, its pace, its frontages, its benches and its shade, not by the count of destinations. A measure built around destinations will systematically undervalue them.

The danger is sharpest when the index ceases to be a description and becomes a target. A city told to raise its score can do so cheaply by permitting a few more shops near existing homes, while the hostile road that separates residents from those shops remains untouched. The number improves; the walk does not. None of this argues for abandoning measurement. It argues for remembering what the measurement leaves out, and for treating a high score as the beginning of an inquiry into a street rather than its conclusion.

CAT-VARC-P03-003-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The passage implies that, before walkability indices became common, walking was widely regarded in planning as
  1. (A)a fallback way of getting about for people who had no option of driving.
  2. (B)the most important consideration in the design of new residential districts.
  3. (C)a form of exercise that estate agents promoted in order to sell homes in the suburbs.
  4. (D)a leisure pursuit with little bearing on how people reached shops and schools.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 says the indices gave walking, 'long treated as what people did when they could not drive', a place in planning arithmetic. Walking was thus seen as a fallback.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says many districts were designed 'as if nobody would ever arrive on foot', the opposite of walking being the main consideration.

Why not (C): Estate agents are said to quote index figures now; nothing suggests they promoted walking as exercise before the indices existed.

Why not (D): The passage says walking was treated as what people did 'when they could not drive', that is, as transport of last resort, not as leisure.

Common misconception: Missing the phrase 'long treated as' and reading the later enthusiasm back into the past.

Difficulty: The inference rests on one phrase in paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-003-Q2 · Except / not supported · MediumMCQ
Each of the following is a claim the author makes about walkability indices EXCEPT that they
  1. (A)rest on an assumption that the main purpose of walking is to reach some destination or other.
  2. (B)can be raised by a city without making the experience of walking in it any better than before.
  3. (C)tend to overstate the number of shops, schools and stops that lie within a short walk of a typical home.
  4. (D)have helped to bring walking into the calculations that planners use when they lay out districts.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The author never questions the accuracy of the counts. The complaint in paragraph 2 is that an accurate count of proximity still ignores 'the walk itself'. The other three claims appear in paragraphs 1, 4 and 3.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says the indices' blindness 'reflects an assumption that walking is chiefly a means of reaching places'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says a city can raise its score by adding shops while the hostile road remains: 'The number improves; the walk does not.'

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says the indices 'gave walking ... a place in the arithmetic of planning', so the author does make this claim.

Common misconception: Assuming that a critic of the indices must think their figures are wrong, when the author's point is that they measure the wrong thing.

Difficulty: The wrong options are paraphrased from three different paragraphs, and the correct answer is a plausible criticism that the author does not make: rejecting it requires seeing that the critique concerns what is measured, not how accurately. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-003-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim that two districts with identical scores can offer entirely different experiences of walking?
  1. (A)Many residents of districts with high index scores continue to own cars and drive for most of their journeys.
  2. (B)Homes in districts with high index scores tend to sell for more than similar homes in districts with low scores.
  3. (C)Narrow pavements beside fast traffic are found in about as many high-scoring districts as low-scoring ones.
  4. (D)Across a large sample of districts, residents' ratings of how pleasant their streets are to walk along closely match the districts' index scores.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The author's claim is that proximity scores do not track the experience of the walk. Evidence that experienced pleasantness closely tracks the score would suggest that districts with equal scores tend to offer similar experiences, undermining the claim.

Why not (A): Car ownership says nothing about whether the walks that are taken feel different in districts with the same score.

Why not (B): Prices bear on how the scores are used by estate agents, not on whether equal scores can hide different walking experiences.

Why not (C): If hostile streets occur regardless of score, the score fails to capture the experience, which supports rather than weakens the author.

Common misconception: Picking the traffic option because it is on topic, without noticing that it supports the author.

Difficulty: One wrong option strengthens the claim and must be told apart from the correct answer by direction; the others are on topic but irrelevant. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-003-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
On the author's view, a city that wished to improve walking in a district, and not merely its walkability score, should begin by
  1. (A)abandoning composite indices and relying instead on residents' own descriptions of what their streets are like.
  2. (B)studying the routes residents actually use between their homes and nearby destinations, and the conditions they meet along them.
  3. (C)raising the share of homes that lie near public transport, since reaching transport is what walking is chiefly for.
  4. (D)permitting more shops, clinics and services to open within a short radius of the homes already in the district.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3 locates the goods of walking 'en route, by the quality of the street', and paragraph 4 recommends treating a score as 'the beginning of an inquiry into a street'. Examining the actual routes and their conditions follows from both.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this argues for abandoning measurement'; the author wants a score treated as the beginning of an inquiry, not discarded.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 identifies the belief that walking is 'chiefly a means of reaching places' as the assumption behind the indices' blindness; the author does not hold it.

Why not (D): This is what paragraph 4 describes as the cheap way to raise the score while 'the hostile road ... remains untouched'; it improves the number, not the walk.

Common misconception: Choosing to abandon the indices, which overstates the author's critique, or to add destinations, which is the very move the author warns about.

Difficulty: Each wrong option reflects a position that appears in the passage (the score-raising move, abandoning measurement, the destination assumption); the correct answer requires combining paragraphs 3 and 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P03-004 · Cities and space · passage Medium · 429 words · 4 questions

In many large cities, a good part of the open space created in recent decades does not belong to the public at all. Developers are permitted to build taller or larger than the rules would otherwise allow on condition that they provide a plaza, an arcade or a garden that anyone may enter. The arrangement looks like an elegant bargain: the city gains open space without paying for it, and the developer gains floor area worth far more than the ground surrendered. Critics have long complained that the resulting spaces are often bleak, hidden or quietly discouraging, with benches too narrow to sit on and guards who appear the moment anyone lingers.

The usual form of the complaint is that these spaces are private, and that private space can never be truly public. This puts the matter in the wrong place. Ownership, by itself, settles very little. Many publicly owned squares are fenced at night, patrolled against skateboarders and stripped of seating to deter people who might sleep there, while some privately owned courtyards are generous, well kept and genuinely welcoming. If the test of publicness were the name on the title deed, we would have to call the first kind more public than the second, which is absurd.

What makes a space public is better described as a set of permissions: the right to be there without a reason, to stay without buying anything, to gather with others, to hand out a leaflet or to hold a placard. These permissions are exactly what private management is tempted to withdraw, not out of malice but because each of them is a small risk to the owner and none of them earns anything. A plaza can be open to all in the formal sense while being governed by rules, often unpublished, that make it public only for the well dressed and the briefly passing. The question to ask is therefore not who owns a space but who writes its rules, and to whom the writers must answer.

Seen this way, the bargain need not be abandoned; it needs to be completed. A city that trades floor area for open space is entitled to specify not only the dimensions of the plaza but the permissions that come with it, to publish them at the entrance, and to enforce them against owners as firmly as it enforces its building codes. Where it fails to do so, it has not acquired public space at all. It has paid, in floor area, for something that looks like a square and behaves like a lobby.

CAT-VARC-P03-004-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, developers come to provide plazas, arcades and gardens open to the public because
  1. (A)the city pays them for the ground at a price well above what it would fetch on the open market.
  2. (B)building codes in most large cities require every new tower to include some open space at its base.
  3. (C)they are allowed to build larger or taller than the rules would otherwise permit in return.
  4. (D)open spaces attract tenants and shoppers, and so add to the rents that the developer can later charge.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1: 'Developers are permitted to build taller or larger than the rules would otherwise allow on condition that they provide a plaza, an arcade or a garden that anyone may enter.'

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says the city 'gains open space without paying for it'; the developer is compensated in floor area, not money.

Why not (B): The passage describes a voluntary exchange 'on condition that' open space is provided, not a requirement imposed on every building.

Why not (D): No commercial motive of this kind is mentioned; the passage names the extra floor area as what the developer gains.

Common misconception: Assuming a commercial motive from general knowledge rather than reading the stated bargain.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-004-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that, in the author's view, private managers tend to withdraw the permissions listed in paragraph 3 chiefly because
  1. (A)those permissions bring the owner a little risk and no income, rather than because owners dislike the public.
  2. (B)planning law in most cities forbids protest and leafleting on land that remains in private ownership.
  3. (C)few people who use such plazas actually wish to stay in them, meet others there or hand out leaflets.
  4. (D)owners regard the people who would linger or protest in their plazas with open hostility and contempt.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 says the permissions are withdrawn 'not out of malice but because each of them is a small risk to the owner and none of them earns anything'. The motive is a calculation of risk and return.

Why not (B): The passage mentions no such law; it says the permissions are withdrawn by management, often through 'unpublished' rules.

Why not (C): The passage gives no evidence about what users want; guards who appear 'the moment anyone lingers' imply that some people do wish to stay.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the permissions are withdrawn 'not out of malice', which rules out hostility as the explanation.

Common misconception: Choosing hostility because the plazas are described as discouraging, and missing 'not out of malice'.

Difficulty: The correct answer paraphrases a contrast in paragraph 3; the hostility wrong option tempts readers who infer motive from effect. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-004-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ
The author's attitude towards the practice of trading extra floor area for open space is best described as
  1. (A)weary indifference, because ownership settles very little and the resulting spaces will be much the same either way.
  2. (B)qualified approval: the exchange is sound in principle but worthless unless the city secures and enforces permissions.
  3. (C)warm enthusiasm, because the city obtains open space without spending any public money and owners keep it well maintained.
  4. (D)outright rejection, because a space in private ownership can never become genuinely public whatever rules are attached to it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says 'the bargain need not be abandoned; it needs to be completed' by specifying, publishing and enforcing permissions. Without that, the city has paid for 'something that looks like a square and behaves like a lobby'. This is support conditional on enforcement.

Why not (A): That ownership 'settles very little' leads the author to focus on rules, not to indifference; paragraph 4 makes firm demands of cities.

Why not (C): The author accepts the critics' account of bleak, discouraging plazas and says a city that does not enforce permissions 'has not acquired public space at all'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says this complaint 'puts the matter in the wrong place', and paragraph 4 says the bargain 'need not be abandoned'.

Common misconception: Reading the sharp final sentence as rejection of the whole practice.

Difficulty: The tone is mixed: a sharp critique of current practice within acceptance of the bargain itself; one wrong option misreads 'settles very little'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-005 · Cities and space · passage Medium · 418 words · 4 questions

Few settlement forms have been as consistently disparaged by writers as the suburb. For the better part of a century, novelists, critics and social scientists have described it in a nearly fixed vocabulary: monotonous rows of identical houses, lawns tended out of anxiety rather than pleasure, a life of commuting, shopping and television in which neighbours are near but never known. The suburb, in this account, is where individuality goes to be smoothed away. The striking thing about the indictment is not its severity but its uniformity, which ought to make us suspicious. A form of life chosen by many millions of households can hardly be as simple as the language used to dismiss it.

Part of the indictment is sound. A district built around the car leaves those who cannot drive, the young, the old and the poor, stranded in a landscape in which nothing is within reach on foot. Zoning that separates homes from shops and workplaces by law multiplies journeys and empties the streets of the casual traffic through which neighbours become familiar. These are real costs, and they fall unevenly. But they are the costs of particular design decisions, and the critique of the suburb has rarely been content to remain at that level.

Its deeper charge is cultural, and here it is weakest. It reads sameness of form as sameness of life, as if a street of similar houses must contain similar people living similar days; anyone who has knocked on the doors of such a street knows otherwise. It also tends to treat the wish for a garden, a quiet bedroom and a door one can close as a failure of imagination, when for families leaving cramped flats these were precisely the goods that city life had denied them. The critics, many of whom lived by choice in the centres of large cities, were often describing a preference they did not share as a pathology from which others suffered.

None of this requires us to admire the suburb, or to ignore the costs of a pattern of building that consumes land and fuel at a great rate. It requires only that criticism be aimed at what can be changed. The desire for privacy and space is not a defect to be corrected; the absence of a shop within walking distance is. A suburb with a small centre, mixed uses and streets that a child can safely cross would answer most of the sound objections without asking anyone to want a different life.

The primary purpose of the passage is to

  2. (B)defend the suburb as a better form of settlement than the dense centre of a large city, in which families are denied space.
  3. (C)show that the residents of suburban streets lead far more varied lives than novelists and critics have claimed.
  4. (D)argue that criticism of the suburb should address its remediable design faults instead of condemning what its residents want.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 questions the uniform indictment, paragraph 2 accepts its practical part, paragraph 3 rejects its cultural part, and paragraph 4 concludes that 'criticism be aimed at what can be changed': design, not desire.

Why not (A): This is the concession in paragraph 2, one part of the argument rather than its purpose.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this requires us to admire the suburb', and the passage never ranks the suburb above the city centre.

Why not (C): The variety of suburban lives is one point in paragraph 3; the passage uses it within a larger argument about where criticism should aim, so the option is too narrow.

Common misconception: Taking the defence of suburban preferences in paragraph 3 for a defence of the suburb as such.

Difficulty: Two wrong options are accurate details; the correct answer requires holding the concession and the rejection together. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-006 · Cities and space · passage Difficult · 473 words · 4 questions

Arguments about gentrification tend to collapse into a single question: are the people who lived in a neighbourhood forced out when wealthier newcomers arrive? The question has the merit of being answerable, at least in principle. One can follow households over time, count how many leave, and compare the rate with that in neighbourhoods that have not changed. Suppose such a count found that long-standing residents of a gentrifying district left no faster than residents of similar districts elsewhere. Many would conclude that the harm of gentrification had been exaggerated, and that what remained of the complaint was mere nostalgia.

That conclusion would be premature, because it assumes that the only way to lose a neighbourhood is to leave it. Consider the resident who stays. The grocer who extended credit has been replaced by a shop whose prices assume another income; the bench outside it, once a place to sit for an afternoon, now draws the attention of a security guard; the park is the same park, but the conduct expected in it has quietly changed, and complaints to the police now come from people she does not know, about behaviour that was ordinary a decade ago. She has not moved, and no count of departures will register her, yet the place in which she lives has been withdrawn from her as surely as if she had. To call this nostalgia is to mistake a loss of standing for a loss of scenery.

The difficulty is that the language best suited to describing this loss, the language of a neighbourhood's 'character', is also the language most readily turned to the opposite purpose. Affluent districts invoke their character to resist new housing, to keep out flats that might bring poorer residents, to preserve a streetscape that functions, whatever its owners intend, as a barrier. If character means the look of the buildings and the tone of the streets, then the long-standing resident and the exclusionary homeowner are making the same argument, and it is not one that deserves much sympathy.

The way out is to locate character not in the fabric of a place but in the relations it sustains: who is able to use it, on what terms, and with what claim to be heard. On this view, what the staying resident has lost is not a familiar shopfront but the practical standing she once had in her own street, and what the exclusionary homeowner defends is not a set of relations at all but the absence of people who might form new ones. The distinction will not settle every case, and it asks more of planners than a count of departures does. But it allows us to take the quieter harms of gentrification seriously without lending our sympathy to every resident who would prefer that nothing near them ever changed.

  4. (D)prove that complaints about gentrification amount to little more than nostalgia for a vanished streetscape.

Why not (D): This is the conclusion that, in paragraph 1, many would draw, which paragraph 2 calls 'premature' and answers with the staying resident.

Common misconception: Choosing the nostalgia option, which is the reported view the author rejects.

Difficulty: Requires separating the reliability of the count, which is not disputed, from the scope of what it measures. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-007 · Cities and space · passage Medium · 598 words · 5 questions

The familiar story of urbanisation runs in one direction. People leave the countryside for the city, struggle for a generation in its least desirable quarters, and are eventually absorbed, their children speaking the city's idiom and regarding the village as a place of grandparents and holidays. Migration, on this account, is a passage with a beginning and an end, and the only question worth asking about a migrant is how far along it he has come. The story is not false; it describes what happened to many families in many countries. But it has acquired the status of a norm, against which other patterns are measured and found wanting, and this has made it difficult to see a pattern that is, in much of the world, at least as common.

A large number of those who work in cities never complete the journey the story describes. They come for a season of construction or a few years in a factory, return for the harvest, a wedding or an illness in the family, and come back again, sometimes to the same city and sometimes to another. Their earnings pay school fees and debts in the village; the village holds their land, their elders and, often, their spouses and children. A household can live this way for decades, and many do. Seen through the one-way story, such people are migrants who have not yet settled, a transitional population whose double life is a symptom of incomplete integration. Seen on its own terms, the pattern looks rather different: a way of spreading risk across two places, so that a lost job in the city or a failed crop in the village need not be ruinous, because the other place remains.

The distinction matters because city governments have tended to act on the first reading. A resident whose household is registered elsewhere, whose stay is irregular, and whose address is a worksite or a shared rented room is easily treated as a visitor: excluded from subsidised housing, from schools for the children who come along, from the electoral roll, and from the attention of politicians who answer to those who are on it. The exclusion then produces the very evidence that justifies it. Denied a secure foothold in the city, the circular migrant has every reason to keep the village in reserve, and the persistence of his village ties is then cited as proof that he never really belonged.

It would be a mistake to romanticise circulation in its turn. Some who move back and forth would settle in the city if they could, and return to the village only because the city offers them nothing to stay for; for them, circularity is less a strategy than a sentence. Others genuinely prefer a life lived in two places and would keep it even if the city were more welcoming. The difficulty is that from the outside the two groups look identical, and a policy designed for one will misjudge the other.

What follows is not a single programme but a change in the condition attached to belonging. A city that made its entitlements portable, that allowed a worker to enrol his children in school without proving permanent residence, and that encouraged cheap rental housing suited to stays of months rather than decades would serve both groups at once. It would allow those who wish to settle to do so, and those who wish to circulate to circulate without penalty. What it would cease to do is demand that a person surrender one home as the price of being recognised in another.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 never compares wages; it explains circulation by the security of having 'the other place' when one fails.

Why not (C): This is the one-way reading of paragraph 1, which paragraph 2 contrasts with the pattern seen on its own terms.

Common misconception: Choosing the one-way reading because it is described in the same paragraph.

CAT-VARC-P03-008 · Cities and space · passage Difficult · 594 words · 5 questions

In the language of property, home and ownership have become almost interchangeable. To own the place one lives in is to 'have a home'; to rent is to be on the way to one, or to have failed to arrive. The equation is so familiar that it seems less a claim than a definition, and policies built on it, from subsidised mortgages to the sale of public housing to its tenants, present themselves not as choices among ways of housing people but as the extension of home itself to those who lack it.

Yet if one asks what people mean when they say that a place feels like home, the answers rarely mention title. They mention routines that have settled into the rooms, objects arranged by one's own hand, the confidence that one will be there next year, the freedom to decide who comes in, the ability to paint a wall or plant a window box or keep a dog. Home, in this sense, is less a thing one possesses than something one does, a practice of shaping a space and being shaped by it over time. And a practice of this kind needs two conditions: enough control over the space to shape it, and enough security of tenure to make the shaping worthwhile.

Neither condition is the same as ownership, though ownership has usually supplied both. A tenant on a twelve-month lease who may be asked to leave at short notice, who must seek permission to hang a picture, and whose rent can rise without limit, has good reason not to invest himself in his rooms; he is camping, however long he stays. But the cause of his condition is not that he rents. It is that his tenancy is short, revocable and hedged with prohibitions. Where tenancies are long, rents predictable and tenants free to alter their surroundings within reason, renters make homes much as owners do, while an owner whose mortgage payments consume most of his income, and who fears that a lost job means a lost house, may know the very precariousness that the equation reserves for tenants.

None of this means that ownership confers nothing distinctive. The owner can knock down a wall, not merely paint it; he can leave the house to his children; he answers to no landlord. A tenant, however secure, lives in a space whose final shape is decided by someone else. These are real goods, and it would be odd to deny that many people want them for good reasons. The objection is not to ownership but to the assumption that it is the only door into home, an assumption that directs public effort towards turning tenants into owners while leaving the conditions of tenancy, under which a large and often growing share of urban households live, largely untouched.

The cost of that assumption is not only sentimental. A household that expects to move at a landlord's word does not join the school committee, does not plant a tree it will not see grow, does not learn the names of neighbours it may lose within the year. Schools, associations and local shops all depend on residents who expect to stay long enough for their efforts to bear fruit. The practices through which people make a home are continuous with those through which they make a neighbourhood, so that insecurity inside the door becomes thinness of attachment outside it. To treat home as a matter of title is therefore to misunderstand not only what renters lack but what cities lose when they lack it.

CAT-VARC-P03-009 · Family and generations · passage Easy · 469 words · 4 questions

Most people in the modern West assume that a name is a fixed possession, given once at birth and carried, with perhaps one change at marriage, until death. The assumption is so deep that we rarely notice it is an assumption at all, yet in a wide range of societies a person's name has been expected to change over a lifetime, and one of the commonest occasions for the change is the birth of a child. When a man becomes known as the father of his first child, or a woman as the mother of hers, anthropologists speak of teknonymy: naming a parent through the offspring. The Arabic custom of addressing a man as Abu, 'father of', followed by the name of his eldest son, is perhaps the most familiar instance, but the practice in various forms has been recorded on several continents.

It is tempting to read teknonymy as a simple sign that children are prized, and there is something in this. But the more interesting point is what the custom reveals about how such societies understand a person. In a system of fixed names, identity is treated as something an individual owns from the start, and relationships are added to it later, like rooms built onto a house. In a teknonymous system the order is reversed: a person becomes fully visible in social life only by standing in a relationship; the new name announces not that the individual has changed but that their position in a web of kin has changed. One is addressed less as a self than as a point where generations meet.

This also explains a feature that puzzles outsiders. In some communities it becomes mildly rude to use the old personal name of an adult who has a child, much as it would be odd for us to call a judge by a childhood nickname in court. The personal name has not been forbidden; it has simply been outranked by a title that carries more social information. Where the practice extends further, so that a grandparent is named after a grandchild, a person's name can track their rise through the generations, and the most senior people come to be known almost entirely through their descendants.

None of this should be romanticised, for a system that names people through their children can be hard on those who have none, who may remain, in the language of address, permanently junior. Nor is the Western fixed name as individualistic as it looks, since a surname itself announces a lineage. The contrast is one of emphasis rather than of kind, but the emphasis matters. To change a person's name at the birth of a child is to state, every time that person is greeted, that adulthood is not a private achievement but a place in a sequence.

CAT-VARC-P03-010 · Family and generations · passage Easy · 432 words · 4 questions

National accounts were designed to measure what passes through markets, and for most of their history they have counted the meal bought in a restaurant but not the meal cooked at home, the hour a paid nurse spends with an elderly patient but not the hour a daughter spends with her mother. The omission was never a secret: early statisticians acknowledged it and set it aside as a practical matter, since household work was hard to observe and there was no price at which to record it. The result, however, was a picture of the economy in which a large share of the work that keeps people alive appeared to be no work at all.

In recent decades, time-use surveys, in which people record how they spend each part of a day, have made it possible to estimate how many hours households devote to cooking, cleaning and care. Turning those hours into money, however, requires a choice between methods that rest on different assumptions. One method, replacement cost, asks what it would cost to hire someone to do the same tasks. Another, opportunity cost, asks what the person doing the work could have earned in paid employment during those hours. The two methods can produce very different totals, and they can rank the same work differently: an hour of childcare by a surgeon is worth a great deal on the second method and rather little on the first.

Critics sometimes take this disagreement as proof that the whole exercise is arbitrary, but that conclusion is too quick. Every figure in national accounts rests on conventions, and the fact that a convention must be chosen does not make zero, the convention long in use, the neutral one. Recording household care at zero is also a valuation, and almost certainly the least accurate available. Estimates, even rough ones, have changed how policy debates can be conducted: they make it harder to describe cuts to public care services as savings when the work has simply moved into homes.

There is, nonetheless, a limit to what a price can capture. Both methods treat care as a bundle of tasks that could in principle be handed to someone else. Much of it can be. But part of what a family member provides is the fact that it is they who provide it, and that element has no market equivalent because it cannot be bought. A figure for unpaid care is therefore best understood as a floor rather than a measure: it shows that the work is not nothing, without claiming to say what it is worth.

Why not (B): Pricing by a bought equivalent is the logic of replacement cost; the restaurant meal in paragraph 1 illustrates what accounts count.

Why not (C): Time-use surveys supply the hours; paragraph 2 says turning hours into money then 'requires a choice' of method.

Common misconception: Confusing the two methods, which are defined in consecutive sentences.

CAT-VARC-P03-011 · Family and generations · passage Medium · 482 words · 4 questions

When European travellers and administrators first described marriages in which the groom's family transferred cattle, cloth or other goods to the bride's family, many of them reached for the language of the market. The bride, they concluded, was being bought. The description had the appeal of simplicity, and it fitted a ready-made story in which other peoples' customs were cruder versions of the observers' own commercial habits. Words such as 'price' and 'purchase' entered official reports, and they shaped how the custom was seen long after closer study had cast doubt on them. The description also misread, in nearly every important respect, what the transfer did.

Consider first where the goods go. In many systems they are not kept by the bride's father as profit but are distributed among a wide circle of her kin, each of whom thereby acquires an interest in the marriage succeeding. Consider next where they go afterwards. Often the cattle received when a daughter marries are the very cattle used to secure a wife for her brother, so that a herd travels from family to family and the transfers form a chain linking households that may never meet. A purchase ends a relationship between buyer and seller; this transfer begins several. Consider, finally, what the transfer secures. In a number of societies what passes with the goods is not the woman herself but the affiliation of her future children to the husband's lineage, which is why, in some of them, the goods must be returned if the marriage ends before any children are born.

On this reading, bridewealth is less a price than a contract between groups, and one that gives the bride's family a continuing stake in how she is treated. A family that has shared the goods out among its members, and may have to gather them again in order to return them, has reason both to support the marriage and to intervene when it goes badly. The goods, in other words, work less like a payment than like a pledge, and a pledge held by many hands is harder to ignore than one held by a single man.

It would be naive, however, to treat this as the whole story. Where cash has replaced cattle and the sums demanded have risen with the incomes of urban grooms, the transfer can begin to behave much more like a price: it is negotiated against the bride's education or earning power, it is kept by a narrower set of relatives, and young men may delay marriage for years in order to raise it. Critics who see a market in such arrangements are not simply repeating the old error. The lesson is not that the travellers were wrong and the custom is innocent, but that the meaning of a transfer depends on the web of obligations around it, and that when the web thins, the meaning changes.

CAT-VARC-P03-011-Q3 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ

  1. (A)wholehearted agreement, since their judgement is extended to bridewealth in every setting.
  2. (B)qualified acceptance: their judgement is granted for such cases without the older verdict being revived.
  3. (C)studied neutrality, because the author declines to say whether their judgement has any merit.
  4. (D)dismissive, because they are seen as repeating the early travellers' error in a modern form.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The author concedes that in cash-based, narrowly held transfers the payment 'can begin to behave much more like a price' and that the critics 'are not simply repeating the old error', while insisting that the lesson is not that 'the travellers were wrong and the custom is innocent' in general but that meaning depends on context.

Why not (A): The author limits the market reading to settings where 'the web thins'; paragraphs 2 and 3 reject it for the older form.

Why not (C): The author explicitly credits the critics in paragraph 4, so this is not neutrality.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says these critics 'are not simply repeating the old error', which rules out dismissal.

Common misconception: Missing the negation in 'not simply repeating the old error' and reading the author as dismissive.

Difficulty: The attitude is carried by a negated phrase and a contrastive final sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-012 · Family and generations · passage Medium · 464 words · 4 questions

Few claims in social history have travelled as far as the idea that childhood was invented. In its best-known form, put forward in the middle of the twentieth century, it held that medieval Europe had no notion of childhood as a distinct stage of life. Children, on this account, passed almost directly from infancy into the company of adults, working, drinking and dressing as they did; painters showed them as miniature men and women; and parents, facing high infant mortality, refrained from investing much feeling in creatures who might not survive. Childhood as we know it, protected, sentimentalised and set apart, was a discovery of the early modern centuries.

The thesis was a brilliant provocation, and much of it has not survived. Later historians, reading letters, court records, sermons and the evidence of excavated objects, assembled a large body of material showing that medieval parents grieved for dead children, bought or made toys for living ones, and recognised that the young needed particular kinds of care. The argument from paintings proved especially weak. Artistic conventions are not photographs, and an age that painted saints with golden discs around their heads was not simply reporting what it saw. To infer an absence of feeling from an absence of naturalistic portraiture is to mistake a style for a sentiment.

Yet it would be a mistake to conclude that nothing changed. The critics established that medieval people knew children were different and loved them; they did not establish that childhood occupied the same place in social life. What the early modern period did see was the gradual spread of institutions that sorted the young by age and held them apart from adult work: schools divided into classes, a growing literature addressed specifically to children, and eventually laws fixing the ages below which a person could not be employed or married. None of these changes was sudden, and none reached every class or region at once; the children of the poor worked beside adults long after the children of the prosperous had been sent to school. Childhood as a feeling may be ancient. Childhood as a segregated, age-graded stage, organised by institutions, is far more recent.

Seen this way, the original thesis and its critics were partly talking past each other. One side asked whether people recognised and cared for children; the other, without always saying so, asked how societies arranged the passage from birth to adult status. This is why the debate could run for so long without resolution: each side could cite evidence the other did not dispute, because each was answering a different question. The second question is the more fruitful, because it leaves room for the observation that every society has children but not every society has childhood in the same form.

Why not (C): The passage makes no claim about how much artists cared for different subjects; the saints illustrate convention, not preference.

CAT-VARC-P03-013 · Family and generations · passage Medium · 446 words · 4 questions

Discussions of population ageing tend to begin with a single number. The old-age dependency ratio divides the number of people above a fixed age, usually sixty-five, by the number of people of working age, conventionally fifteen to sixty-four. As birth rates fall and lives lengthen the ratio rises, and a rising ratio is read as a growing burden: fewer shoulders, it is said, bearing the weight of more pensioners. The image is vivid and the arithmetic is simple, which is precisely why the measure deserves more suspicion than it usually receives.

The ratio rests on two equations that are rarely stated. The first is that everyone between fifteen and sixty-four supports others and everyone above sixty-five is supported by them. Neither half is true. Large numbers of people of working age are in education, unemployed, caring full-time for relatives or otherwise outside paid work, while many people beyond the threshold continue to earn, to pay taxes and, perhaps most importantly, to provide unpaid care. In many families it is grandparents whose looking after small children allows both parents to hold jobs; on the ratio's accounting, those grandparents appear only as a cost. The same error runs in the other direction for the many people in their fifties who have left paid work early through illness or redundancy, and who depend on others well before the line is reached.

The second equation is that a given age means the same thing in every generation. A sixty-five-year-old today in most countries can expect to live considerably longer, and in better health, than a sixty-five-year-old of the generation in which the threshold was fixed. To keep the line at sixty-five as life expectancy rises is to classify as old an ever larger share of people who are, by the standards that originally justified the line, not old at all. Some demographers have proposed measuring age prospectively, by the years a person can expect still to live rather than the years already lived; on such measures, populations age considerably more slowly than the headline ratio suggests.

None of this means that ageing is costless. The final years of a long life often bring a need for intensive medical and personal care, and as more people reach advanced old age that need grows in ways no redefinition can wish away. The objection is not to concern but to calibration. A measure that counts heads by birthday rather than activity by circumstance will tend to exaggerate the problem in its general form and to obscure it in the specific form, very late life, in which it is most acute.

CAT-VARC-P03-014 · Family and generations · passage Medium · 427 words · 4 questions

For much of the twentieth century, anthropologists sorted the ties they recorded into two kinds. Some were 'real', resting on birth and descent; others were 'fictive', a convenient label for godparents, sworn brothers, adopted children and the many other relations that people treated as kinship but that did not, so the observer thought, rest on blood. The distinction seemed merely descriptive, and it took some time to see that it carried a theory with it: that the true foundation of kinship is biological, and that everything else is imitation.

The difficulty is that many of the people being described did not share the theory. In a number of societies, people become kin by eating food grown on the same land, by living in the same house, or by being nursed at the same breast. Under Islamic law, for instance, children nursed by the same woman are barred from marrying one another, much as siblings by birth would be; the tie created by milk is not a metaphor for kinship but a form of it. In such places, ties made in these ways may carry the same obligations of help, hospitality and mourning as ties of birth. Where relatedness is understood to accumulate through feeding and sharing over years, to call such ties fictive is not to describe them but to overrule the people who live them.

This is not an argument that biology is irrelevant. Birth is, almost everywhere, one of the most powerful ways of reckoning who belongs to whom, and an attempt to dissolve it entirely into choice would be its own kind of distortion. The point is narrower: biology is one idiom of relatedness among several, and which idioms count, and how much, varies from place to place. Nor is the West an exception that proves the biological rule. Adoption, the blended families formed by remarriage, and the arrangements made possible by donated eggs and sperm all require people to decide, often painfully, whether a tie of birth or a tie of care should prevail when the two come apart.

Seen this way, kinship is something people do as well as something they are born into. The older vocabulary was not useless, since it pointed to real differences in the ways ties are formed and maintained. Its error lay in treating one culture's answer to the question of what makes people kin as the standard against which every other answer was to be measured, and in mistaking that answer for a fact of nature rather than a choice that other peoples had made differently.

  4. (D)metaphorical, because it is modelled on the bond between brothers and sisters.

Paragraph 1 says the older vocabulary called 'fictive' all relations 'that did not ... rest on blood'. A milk tie is not a tie of birth, so it would have been fictive; paragraph 2 objects to exactly that.

Why not (D): Metaphor is not one of the two categories of the older scheme, and the author denies that the milk tie is 'a metaphor for kinship'.

Common misconception: Confusing the author's view of the milk tie with how the older vocabulary would have classed it.

Difficulty: Applies a definition from paragraph 1 to an example from paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-015 · Family and generations · passage Difficult · 629 words · 5 questions

Every few years a new generation is given a name, and with the name comes a character. One cohort is said to be idealistic and later complacent, another cynical, another anxious, entitled or unusually attached to its telephones. The labels are used by marketers, journalists and employers with a confidence that would be remarkable in any other classification of human beings. We would hesitate to say that everyone born under a given star sign shares a temperament; we say something not very different, and with far less hesitation, about everyone born within a given span of fifteen years. The labels are also remarkably durable: once a cohort has been named and described, the description tends to follow it into middle age, revised only when a newer cohort arrives to take its place as the subject of worry.

The trouble begins with a problem of method that social scientists have long recognised. When one group of people differs from another, the difference may reflect their age (the young almost everywhere change jobs more often than the middle-aged), the period in which they are observed (everyone, of whatever age, was affected by a recession or a new technology), or the cohort into which they were born, meaning a lasting imprint left by the conditions of their early years. Only the last is a generational effect in the strict sense. But the three are bound together by arithmetic: a person's age is simply the current year minus the year of birth, so that once any two are known the third is fixed. No set of observations can by itself pull them apart; some assumption from outside the data is always required. Much of what is confidently attributed to a generation turns out, on inspection, to be what people of that age have always done, or what people of every age were doing at the time.

A second difficulty concerns boundaries. The years at which one generation is said to end and the next to begin are chosen by convention, and different sources disagree about them by several years. A person born on the last day of one span and a person born on the first day of the next are assigned different characters, while two people born fourteen years apart within one span are assigned the same. Differences within a labelled cohort, by class, region, education and much else, are typically far larger than the differences between cohorts, which is to say that the label groups people along the axis on which they least differ.

None of this shows that generations are a fiction. There is a serious idea buried under the marketing: that people who come of age during a war, a depression or a political upheaval may carry its mark for life, and may differ in lasting ways from those who met the same events earlier or later in their lives. Such effects have been found, and they are not trivial. But they are anchored to events rather than to calendars; they mark some members of a cohort far more deeply than others, depending on where and how the event touched them; and they rarely produce a single character that an entire cohort shares.

Why, then, do the calendar labels persist? Part of the answer is that they are convenient, turning a tangle of causes into a story with protagonists. Part of it is that they flatter, inviting each cohort to see itself as distinctive and its elders or juniors as the problem. The cost of the story is easy to miss. When young adults find housing unaffordable or work insecure, attributing their situation to a generational temperament converts a question about economic arrangements into a question about character, and a question about character has no obvious policy answer.

CAT-VARC-P03-015-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)explain why marketers, journalists and employers have come to depend so heavily on labels for each new generation.
  2. (B)argue that calendar-based generation labels misplace the causes of difference, while granting a narrower, event-anchored notion of generation.
  3. (C)show that differences in age and period matter less for explaining how people behave than differences of class and region do.
  4. (D)demonstrate that genuine differences between generations can never be detected in survey data, whatever methods researchers choose to use.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraphs 2 and 3 give two reasons the labels mislead (confounded effects, arbitrary boundaries), paragraph 4 concedes a 'serious idea' anchored to events, and paragraph 5 explains the labels' persistence and cost. The correct answer captures the critique together with the concession.

Why not (A): Why the labels persist is the subject of paragraph 5 only; it is one part of a larger critique, so the option is too narrow.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 compares variation within and between cohorts; the passage never ranks age and period against class and region.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says separating the effects requires 'some assumption from outside the data', not that detection is impossible, and paragraph 4 says cohort effects 'have been found'.

Common misconception: Overstating paragraph 2's method problem into a claim of impossibility.

Difficulty: The wrong options are each tied to one paragraph; the correct answer requires the concession in paragraph 4 as well. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-015-Q2 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
A survey carried out in a single year finds that people then in their twenties change jobs more often than people then in their fifties. On the reasoning of paragraph 2, the survey
  1. (A)would separate age from cohort effects if it were simply repeated with a much larger and more representative sample.
  2. (B)has shown that the difference is a period effect, since every person surveyed was observed in the same year.
  3. (C)cannot by itself show whether the difference reflects age or a lasting imprint left on each group by its birth cohort.
  4. (D)has established that the difference is an age effect, because paragraph 2 says that young people change jobs more often.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

In one year, period is held constant, so the observed difference must be due to age or cohort, and in a single cross-section age and birth cohort move together exactly. Paragraph 2 says no observations 'can by itself pull them apart; some assumption from outside the data is always required'.

Why not (A): The obstacle is arithmetic ('once any two are known the third is fixed'), so a larger sample of the same design cannot remove it.

Why not (B): When all observations come from one year, the period is the same for both groups, so a period effect cannot explain a difference between them.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 offers the job-changing example only to illustrate what an age effect is; it insists that 'No set of observations can by itself pull them apart'.

Common misconception: Treating the illustrative example in paragraph 2 as a finding, or thinking that more data cures an identification problem.

Difficulty: Requires applying the age, period and cohort logic to a new case and seeing that period is fixed in a single-year survey. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P03-015-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ

CAT-VARC-P03-016 · Family and generations · passage Difficult · 622 words · 5 questions

There is a familiar story about the family in South Asia. Once, it runs, people lived in large joint households: an elderly couple, their married sons with wives and children, perhaps unmarried daughters and a widowed aunt, all sharing one roof, one kitchen and one purse under the authority of the eldest man. Then came cities, salaried jobs and the ideal of the companionate couple, and the joint family began to break up into small units of parents and children. The story is told with pride by those who see the old household as a school of duty and with relief by those who see it as a school of obedience, but both versions agree about the direction of travel. It is repeated by planners, preachers and novelists alike, and it is so familiar that it is rarely asked for its evidence.

The evidence for the story is weaker than its popularity suggests. Much of it comes from counting households at a single moment, and a single moment is a poor guide to a process that unfolds over decades. A joint household is not a permanent structure but a phase. A young couple may live alone for some years; the household becomes joint when a son marries and brings his wife home; it may grow when a second son does the same; and it often divides when the father dies and the brothers set up separate hearths, each of which may in turn become joint a generation later. In a population in which every family followed this cycle exactly, a count taken on any given day would find a great many nuclear households, even though nearly everyone would spend part of life in a joint one. A high proportion of nuclear households, then, is not by itself evidence that the joint family is disappearing.

The wider theory behind the story fares little better. It was long assumed that industry produced the nuclear family, by moving work out of the home and rewarding people who could move to wherever the jobs were. Yet historians of north-western Europe found that small households of parents and children were already common there long before the factories arrived; if anything, the causation may have run partly the other way. A society's household form, it seems, is not simply read off its economy.

This is not to say that nothing has changed. Migration for work has scattered many families across cities and countries, so that brothers who would once have shared a courtyard now live hundreds of kilometres apart. But dispersal is not the same thing as dissolution. Money flows back to parents; decisions about marriages, property and the education of children are still taken together; a son abroad may be more bound by his family's expectations than a son next door. Some families keep a common fund into which brothers in different cities pay, and from which the costs of weddings and illnesses are met. A census that defines the joint family by shared residence will record such a family as several nuclear ones, and will miss the jointness that matters most to its members.

It would be equally mistaken to swing to the opposite view and declare the joint family unchanged. What is being renegotiated, often bitterly, is less the size of the household than the authority within it: whether a daughter-in-law must defer to her mother-in-law, whether elders decide whom the young marry, whether property passes to sons alone. The quarrels are often sharpest where the household is still shared, because shared space turns every question of precedence into a daily one. These are real changes, and they are better described as a transformation of jointness than as its end.
