  3. (C)one of the sons migrates for work to a city hundreds of kilometres away.
  4. (D)the father dies and his sons set up separate hearths of their own.

Answer: (D)

Why not (A): Division of property among daughters is not described as a cause of partition; paragraph 5 mentions inheritance only as a contested question.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says this is when the household 'becomes joint', not when it divides.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says migration brings 'dispersal', which 'is not the same thing as dissolution'.

Common misconception: Confusing the stage at which a household becomes joint with the stage at which it divides.

Difficulty: The answer is one clause of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-017 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Easy · 428 words · 4 questions

It is tempting to treat superstition as a residue of ignorance, a set of mistaken beliefs that education will eventually wash away. The difficulty with this view is that superstition does not distribute itself the way ignorance does. Highly trained surgeons, pilots, stage actors and professional athletes, people who know a great deal about the causal workings of their craft, are often among the most attached to private rituals: a particular pair of socks, a fixed sequence of gestures before a performance, a refusal to say certain words aloud. If superstition were merely a gap in knowledge, these would be the last people we would expect to keep it.

A more useful observation is that superstition gathers where two conditions meet: the outcome matters a great deal, and a significant part of it lies beyond the actor's control. Ethnographers have described fishing communities that use no protective magic when they work in a sheltered lagoon, where skill reliably brings a catch, yet surround voyages into the open sea with elaborate rites. The same people hold the same beliefs about the world; what changes is their exposure to chance. Examinations, sporting finals and opening nights share this structure. Preparation counts, but on the day itself something unaccountable remains, and the ritual fills the space that preparation cannot reach.

What the ritual supplies, on this account, is not a causal lever over the outcome but a way of acting when no further useful action is available. Anxiety in the face of uncertainty tends to seek an outlet, and an outlet that is fixed, brief and familiar is better than one that is improvised, such as a last-minute change to a plan that was sound. A tennis player who bounces the ball exactly the same number of times before every serve is not, in any serious sense, trying to bend the laws of physics. She is marking a boundary between deliberation and execution, and the routine protects the execution from second thoughts.

None of this makes superstition harmless. The same disposition that steadies a performer can lead a patient to choose a charm over a treatment that works, or a trader to mistake a lucky streak for a method. The line falls roughly where the ritual stops occupying the residue of chance and begins to displace action that would genuinely improve the odds. Understood this way, superstition is neither a quaint error to be laughed at nor a hidden wisdom to be celebrated, but a predictable human response to exposure that deserves to be judged by what it crowds out.

CAT-VARC-P03-017-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)defending superstition as a hidden form of practical wisdom that modern societies have been wrong to dismiss.
  2. (B)arguing that education will in time remove superstition by filling the gaps in people's knowledge of causes.
  3. (C)explaining superstition as a response to uncontrollable stakes and proposing that it be judged by what it displaces.
  4. (D)proving that skilled professionals are more superstitious than other people because their work involves greater danger.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 rejects the ignorance view, paragraphs 2 and 3 explain superstition as a response to high stakes that lie partly beyond control, and paragraph 4 sets the criterion: it 'deserves to be judged by what it crowds out'. The correct answer covers both the explanation and the evaluative criterion.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says superstition is 'neither a quaint error to be laughed at nor a hidden wisdom to be celebrated', so this reverses the author's balance.

Why not (B): This is the view paragraph 1 rejects: superstition 'does not distribute itself the way ignorance does'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says such professionals are 'often among the most attached' to rituals, which is offered only to unsettle the ignorance view; nothing is proved about their being more superstitious than everyone else, and danger is not the stated cause.

Common misconception: Picking the option about professionals because paragraph 1 dwells on them, when that example only serves to clear away the ignorance view.

Difficulty: The structure is signposted and the two wrong views are explicitly rejected in the text. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-018 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Easy · 420 words · 4 questions

A common modern objection to traditional mourning customs is that they put grief on a timetable. Many traditions mark fixed days after a death, often a gathering within the first two weeks, another around the fortieth day and a final observance at the first anniversary, and for centuries some communities prescribed the clothes a widow might wear and the months during which she should not appear at celebrations. To a sensibility that prizes authentic feeling, this looks like an intrusion: grief, it is said, is personal, it follows no calendar, and nobody has the right to tell a bereaved person when to stop.

The objection contains a truth, but it misreads what the calendar was for. The fixed days rarely claimed to measure how long sorrow lasts. What they did was settle a set of practical questions that otherwise fall, at the worst possible moment, on the people least able to answer them. When should relatives visit, and when should they leave? What is it acceptable to say? When may the household resume ordinary work without seeming callous? A prescribed sequence answers all of these in advance, so that the bereaved are spared the labour of inventing their own conduct while they are exhausted, and so that neighbours know when their presence is expected.

In this sense the timetable was addressed less to the mourner than to everyone around the mourner, translating an event that isolates one household into a sequence of obligations that the wider community could recognise and discharge. Its main work was coordination. Where such scripts have weakened, the result has not obviously been freer grief. Friends hesitate, unsure whether a call would comfort or intrude; colleagues say nothing for fear of saying the wrong thing; and the bereaved, told that they may grieve in their own way, often find that their own way is a lonely one, because nobody else knows what part to play in it.

This is not an argument for restoring every old rule, nor for pretending that the old forms were uniformly benign. Some customs plainly bore down hardest on those with least power, and a widow who was obliged to wear mourning for years was being policed as much as consoled. The lesson is narrower. A society that abandons shared forms for mourning does not thereby hand grief back to the individual; it removes the cues that let others accompany it. Any replacement worth having would keep that coordinating function while dropping the demand that inner feeling match an outward schedule.

  3. (C)measure how long sorrow normally lasts, so that the bereaved would know when their grief ought to end.
  4. (D)give community elders a means of supervising how each family spent its money after a death.

CAT-VARC-P03-018-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely agree that, where shared mourning scripts have weakened,

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 describes friends who 'hesitate' and colleagues who 'say nothing for fear of saying the wrong thing', because 'nobody else knows what part to play'. Paragraph 4 adds that abandoning shared forms 'removes the cues that let others accompany' grief.

Why not (B): Nothing in the passage says that schedules prolonged sorrow or that grief has become shorter; this is outside its scope.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the result 'has not obviously been freer grief'; the author grants loneliness, not greater authenticity.

Why not (D): The passage describes the absence of scripts, not new rigid rules; this contradicts paragraph 3.

Common misconception: Accepting the half-true option that grief is lonelier, without noticing that its other half (more authentic) is denied.

Difficulty: One wrong option pairs a supported claim with one the author rejects. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-019 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Medium · 446 words · 4 questions

Anthropologists have long described rites of passage as moving in three phases. The person undergoing the rite is first separated from an existing status, then passes through a transitional period in which he or she belongs to neither the old condition nor the new, and is finally incorporated into the community in a new role: adult, married, initiated, ordained. Of these phases the middle one, often called liminal, from the Latin word for a threshold, has attracted by far the most attention, and for good reason. It is there that most of the strange features of such rites are concentrated: seclusion in the forest or a special hut, the shaving of heads, the wearing of identical plain garments, the suspension of names, the imposition of hardship and silence.

A simple reading treats these features as a waiting room, a pause while the real change, the conferral of new status, is prepared offstage. This misses their function. Stripping novices of names, clothing and possessions removes the markers by which they were previously ranked, so that for a time sons of important families and sons of poor ones are indistinguishable. Shared hardship then does two things at once. It makes the novices receptive to instruction, since people deprived of their usual footing listen differently, and it binds them to one another in a way that often outlasts the ritual, creating cohorts whose loyalty cuts across lineage and wealth.

In recent decades, however, the idea has travelled far from its origins, and something has been lost on the way. The liminal phase is now often celebrated as a zone of freedom, creativity and play, and the word is applied to almost any in-between experience: the years after school, a period of unemployment, a long journey abroad. This enthusiasm overlooks how tightly the traditional threshold was managed. Elders set its length, prescribed what novices could eat and say, and decided when it was over. Its ambiguity was real, but it was ambiguity held inside a firm frame, and its whole point was the return.

That frame is what most modern transitions lack. A young person who drifts between education and employment may experience the disorientation of the threshold without its discipline, its companions or, above all, its exit. There is no moment at which the community declares the passage complete and receives the person in a new capacity. None of this implies that the old rites should be revived, since their authority rested on forms of social life that no longer exist. But the comparison suggests that what made liminality productive was not the openness that its modern admirers praise. It was the certainty that the openness would end.

  3. (C)suggest that rites of passage are often cruel to novices and ought to be reformed for that reason.
  4. (D)contrast the transitional phase with the more elaborate ceremonies that mark incorporation.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The sentence before the list says the middle phase has attracted the most attention 'for good reason', and the list begins 'It is there that most of the strange features of such rites are concentrated'. The list supplies that reason.

Why not (B): The list gives typical features; nothing claims that all cultures share every one of them, which would be an extreme reading.

Why not (C): The author never condemns the practices; paragraph 2 goes on to explain their function.

Why not (D): No ceremonies of incorporation are described, so there is nothing for the list to be contrasted with.

Common misconception: Reading a moral judgement into a descriptive list.

Difficulty: The function is announced by the sentence that introduces the list. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-020 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Medium · 450 words · 4 questions

There are two familiar ways of misunderstanding pilgrimage, and they are mirror images of each other. The first treats the journey as mere transport: what matters is arrival at the shrine, the sight of the image or the relic, the bath in the river, and the road is simply the price paid to get there. The second, popular among those who walk old pilgrim routes for reasons they would not call religious, reverses the emphasis. The destination, on this view, is almost a pretext; the real value lies in the walking itself, the days of effort, the strangers met on the way, the slow emptying of the mind.

Each view captures something that pilgrims genuinely report, but both detach two elements that draw their meaning from each other, and neither can be made intelligible without the other. The hardships of the road are not valuable in the abstract. Blistered feet on a hiking holiday are an inconvenience; the same blisters on the way to a sacred place become an offering, a sign of seriousness, a story to be told on return. What converts them is the fixed point towards which they are directed. Equally, the shrine does not possess its full weight for the pilgrim independently of the effort spent reaching it. A visitor who arrives by car on a whim may see exactly what the pilgrim sees and still not have had the pilgrim's experience.

The history of easier travel bears this interdependence out in an unexpected way, and it is worth dwelling on because it runs against a natural expectation. One might have predicted that when roads, railways and buses made distant shrines accessible within hours, the effortful element would simply vanish. In many places it has instead been reintroduced by choice. Pilgrims who have covered most of the distance in comfort often leave the vehicle to walk the final stretch, sometimes barefoot, sometimes on their knees. The effort has been condensed rather than abandoned, as though people sense that arrival without cost would not count as arrival at all.

None of this means that the walker who sets out with no particular reverence is deceived about what he finds. Long days on foot do change people, and the change can be real without being religious. But it is worth noticing what such a walker relinquishes, perhaps without realising it, when the destination becomes optional rather than obligatory. The pilgrim cannot decide halfway that another town would do just as well, and that inability is part of what lends the road its gravity. A journey that could end anywhere is a fine thing; it is simply a different thing from one that must end in a particular place.

  2. (B)The authorities at many shrines now require pilgrims to cover the whole route to them on foot.
  3. (C)Pilgrims have come to value the sight of the shrine far more than any hardship they might meet on the road leading to it.
  4. (D)Pilgrims have chosen to bring back some of the effort, for instance by walking the last part of the way.

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 says the effortful element 'has instead been reintroduced by choice', with pilgrims leaving their vehicles 'to walk the final stretch, sometimes barefoot, sometimes on their knees'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 mentions this only as what 'One might have predicted'; it then says the effort 'has instead been reintroduced by choice'.

Why not (B): No requirement by shrine authorities is mentioned; paragraph 3 says the effort is reintroduced 'by choice'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 describes pilgrims adding hardship back, which is the opposite of valuing arrival alone.

Common misconception: Taking the prediction the author mentions and rejects for what actually happened.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in two adjacent sentences of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-021 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Medium · 440 words · 4 questions

Visitors to shrines that are frequented by people of more than one religion tend to come away with one of two stories. In the first, the shrine is evidence of a deep harmony beneath official differences: here, at the tomb of a holy figure or a spring credited with healing powers, ordinary believers ignore the boundaries their clergy insist on and share a common devotion. In the second, the arrangement is a fragile truce, a curiosity that survives only until politics catches up with it. Both stories are told about sites around the Mediterranean, in the Balkans and across South Asia, and both, I think, misdescribe what sharing usually involves.

Close observation of such places rarely finds a single, blended faith. More often it finds parallel use. One community lights lamps in the evening, another comes on a particular weekday; one group knows the saint by one name and story, another by a different name and a different story, and neither is much troubled by the discrepancy. Custodians, frequently from one community, accept offerings from all and refrain from asking visitors what they believe. The sharing is real, but it consists in a set of practical accommodations rather than in agreement, and it works partly because certain questions are never posed: above all, the question of whose shrine it really is.

This is why the harmony story, attractive as it is, puts the emphasis in the wrong place. The arrangement does not depend on people believing the same thing; it depends on their not being required to settle what they believe about this particular place. Nor does the truce story fare better. Many such sites have been shared for generations, through periods of considerable tension elsewhere, which is hard to square with the idea that their peace is merely a pause. What usually threatens them is not the slow pressure of difference but a sudden demand for definition, when some authority, a government department, a heritage body or a movement seeking to purify practice, insists that the site be registered, restored or claimed as belonging to one tradition alone.

It would be romantic to pretend that these places were free of friction. Custodians could favour their own community, quarrels over offerings were common, and the vagueness that allowed sharing also allowed one side to quietly enlarge its claim. Still, the lesson they offer is more interesting than either of the usual stories. Coexistence, they suggest, need not wait for agreement about ultimate questions. It can rest on a carefully maintained ambiguity, which is a social achievement in its own right and, like most achievements, easier to destroy than to build.

  4. (D)a request by visitors that the custodians explain the meaning of the rites performed at the site.

Answer: (B)

The same sentence explains the phrase: 'when some authority, a government department, a heritage body or a movement seeking to purify practice, insists that the site be registered, restored or claimed as belonging to one tradition alone'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 contrasts the demand with 'the slow pressure of difference', so a gradual build-up is exactly what the phrase does not mean.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says custodians are not asked and do not ask about belief; the phrase is glossed in paragraph 3 as registering or claiming the site for one tradition.

Common misconception: Reading 'definition' as explanation of meaning rather than official assignment of identity.

Difficulty: The phrase is glossed in the sentence where it appears. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-022 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Difficult · 440 words · 4 questions

For much of the twentieth century, social scientists took it nearly for granted that modernisation and religion were locked in a zero-sum relationship. As societies industrialised, urbanised and educated their populations, faith would retreat, first from public life and then from private conviction. When the last decades of the century brought vigorous religious movements on several continents, and when religion returned to the centre of political argument in places where it had supposedly been tamed, many concluded that the secularisation thesis had simply been refuted. Some of its former defenders said as much.

Both the confidence and the recantation, however, rested on treating as one hypothesis what was really a bundle of several. One strand concerns differentiation: the process by which law, medicine, education, science and the economy come to operate by their own criteria, no longer answerable to religious authority. A second concerns decline: a fall in belief, membership and practice among individuals. A third concerns privatisation: the withdrawal of religion from public argument into the domain of personal choice. These claims are logically independent. A society could see religious practice flourish while its hospitals, courts and universities remain entirely outside clerical control, and that combination is in fact common.

Once the strands are separated, the evidence looks different. The decline thesis has fared poorly as a universal law; patterns of belief vary too widely between societies at similar levels of wealth to support it. The privatisation thesis has fared worse still, since religious voices are plainly present in public debate. But differentiation has held up remarkably well, and the point most often missed is that the religious resurgence itself tends to presuppose it. Movements that seek public influence organise as voluntary associations, compete for members, lobby legislatures, commission surveys and appeal to rights. They act, that is, as one set of claimants among many within institutions they do not control, which is precisely the condition that differentiation describes.

It would be too quick to declare the older thesis vindicated by this route. A theory that predicted the fading of faith and got that prediction wrong in much of the world cannot be rescued by pointing to the part that survived, and some of its proponents did expect the strands to move together. The more modest conclusion is that the debate has been conducted at the wrong level of generality. To ask whether the world is becoming secular is to ask a question with no single answer; to ask which institutions have slipped free of religious authority, and on what terms religion now competes for influence within them, is to ask questions that the evidence can actually settle.

CAT-VARC-P03-022-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)demonstrate that religion has been confined to private life in most societies that have modernised.
  2. (B)show that the secularisation thesis was right after all, once the evidence of its critics is properly understood.
  3. (C)explain why religious movements revived in the late twentieth century in societies that were growing wealthier.
  4. (D)argue that the secularisation debate went astray by treating several independent claims as a single thesis.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 states that 'Both the confidence and the recantation' treated 'as one hypothesis what was really a bundle of several'. Paragraph 3 tests each strand separately, and paragraph 4 concludes that 'the debate has been conducted at the wrong level of generality'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says the privatisation thesis 'has fared worse still, since religious voices are plainly present in public debate', which contradicts this.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says 'It would be too quick to declare the older thesis vindicated', so the author explicitly declines this conclusion.

Why not (C): The revival is the starting point in paragraph 1, but the passage never explains its causes; it analyses what the revival does and does not refute.

Common misconception: Choosing the vindication option because paragraph 3 defends differentiation, without reading the refusal in paragraph 4.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option follows from paragraph 3 alone; the concession in paragraph 4 decides it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-022-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely regard a society in which religious attendance is high but courts and hospitals operate free of clerical control as
  1. (A)consistent with the differentiation strand of the thesis while failing to fit the decline strand.
  2. (B)an unusual and unstable combination that the pressures of modernisation will sooner or later break apart.
  3. (C)proof that privatisation has taken place in that society even though decline has plainly not.
  4. (D)evidence that the secularisation thesis has now been refuted in every one of its forms.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 defines differentiation as institutions such as law and medicine operating 'no longer answerable to religious authority', and decline as 'a fall in belief, membership and practice'. High attendance with independent courts and hospitals fits the first and contradicts the second; paragraph 2 calls this combination 'common'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says 'that combination is in fact common', and nothing in the passage predicts it will break apart.

Why not (C): The case says nothing about whether religion is present in public argument, which is what privatisation concerns; the inference has no basis.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the strands are 'logically independent'; such a society refutes decline but fits differentiation, so not every form is refuted.

Common misconception: Running the strands together, which is the very error the passage diagnoses.

Difficulty: Requires applying two of the three definitions to a case and keeping the third out. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-023 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Difficult · 618 words · 5 questions

Many societies have set aside days on which the ordinary order is turned upside down. In the Roman Saturnalia, masters are said to have waited on their slaves at table; in parts of medieval Europe, junior clergy elected a mock bishop and parodied the liturgy; in countless village festivals men dress as women, children give orders to adults, and the powerful are mocked in songs that would be punished at any other time of year. Such festivals of inversion have long puzzled those who study them, because they seem to put the most fundamental arrangements of a society at risk for the sake of a few days of licence.

Two explanations have dominated. The first, often called the safety valve theory, holds that inversion protects the order it mocks. Resentments that accumulate under hierarchy are released in a bounded, harmless form, and when the festival ends everyone returns to their place, relieved and more willing to stay there. The second reverses the judgement. On this view, the festival gives people a glimpse of a world arranged otherwise, keeps alive the memory that rank is not natural, and so contains the seed of real defiance. Supporters of each view can point to cases. Rulers have tolerated and even funded such festivals, which suggests they found them useful; they have also banned them, which suggests they found them dangerous, and on a few occasions a carnival has indeed turned into a riot.

What both explanations share is the assumption that the meaning of inversion is fixed by its form, so that one need only decide, once and for all, whether turning the world upside down conserves it or threatens it. The historical record does not support so tidy a choice, since the same festival, with the same masks and the same songs, has been a harmless frolic in one decade and the occasion of an uprising in the next. The difference lay not in the ritual but in its circumstances: a bad harvest, a disputed tax, a quarrel between factions of the elite that made the mockery of one of them useful to the other. Inversion, in other words, does not carry its own political charge; it takes one from the situation into which it is performed.

The safety valve theory has a further weakness that is easy to overlook because its central image is so vivid. It pictures discontent as a kind of pressure that builds up in a closed vessel and can be let off in measured bursts. There is little reason to think that resentment, which is attached to particular injuries and particular persons, behaves like steam that can be vented without residue; people who have mocked their masters for a day do not obviously feel less aggrieved afterwards, and they may feel more entitled to their grievance for having heard it voiced aloud by a crowd. The image persuades because it is mechanical, not because anyone has observed the mechanism.

A more promising account starts from a simpler observation. To turn a world upside down, one must first show which way up it is. The mock bishop presupposes a real one; the servant who gives orders for a day makes plain who gives them on every other day. Inversion is thus a vivid lesson in hierarchy, and in that sense it does teach the order it reverses. But the lesson cuts both ways, since a ranking that can be so easily reversed in play is shown to be a convention rather than a fact of nature. Which of these lessons is learned depends, again, on what else is happening. That is an unsatisfying conclusion only for those who wanted the festival to have a single meaning.

CAT-VARC-P03-023-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the fact that rulers have both funded and banned festivals of inversion
  1. (A)shows that rulers regarded these festivals as harmless entertainment with no political significance.
  2. (B)has been cited by supporters of each of the two dominant explanations as evidence for their view.
  3. (C)proves that the festivals tended to turn into riots wherever the authorities allowed them to continue for long enough.
  4. (D)indicates that such festivals were supported mainly by the elite and opposed mainly by ordinary people.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says 'Supporters of each view can point to cases': funding 'suggests they found them useful' (the safety valve) and banning 'suggests they found them dangerous' (the subversive view).

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says banning suggests rulers 'found them dangerous', and funding suggests they found them 'useful'; neither implies no significance.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says riots occurred only 'on a few occasions'; nothing ties them to long toleration.

Why not (D): The passage contrasts different responses by rulers; it says nothing about ordinary people opposing the festivals.

Common misconception: Reading the funding and banning as evidence for a single conclusion.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in the last two sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-023-Q5 · Inference · DifficultMCQ

  2. (B)Because inversion displays the hierarchy in a way that can be read as confirming or exposing it, the surrounding situation must decide which reading prevails.

CAT-VARC-P03-024 · Ritual and belief as social practice · passage Medium · 579 words · 5 questions

Why do so many religious and quasi-religious groups ask their members to do things that are difficult, uncomfortable or expensive? Long fasts, pre-dawn prayers, restrictive diets, distinctive dress that invites ridicule, vows that forgo income or marriage: from a narrowly economic point of view these look like pure loss. An influential answer, borrowed from the study of animal behaviour, is that the costs are the point. A signal that is cheap to send can be sent by anyone, and so tells an observer little. A signal that is costly to send, and costlier still for someone who does not mean it, is credible precisely because it is hard to fake.

Applied to ritual, the idea runs as follows. Groups that depend on mutual aid, sharing harvests, lending money or protecting one another, are vulnerable to people who take the benefits of membership without bearing its burdens. Demanding rituals screen such people out. Someone who is willing to rise before dawn for years, or to give up foods that everyone else enjoys, is unlikely to be only nominally attached, and the others can extend trust accordingly. The theory also explains a pattern that has often been remarked upon: groups that lower their demands in the hope of attracting members sometimes find that they lose them instead, as the value of belonging falls along with its cost.

This is a genuinely illuminating account, and it captures something that purely symbolic interpretations of ritual tend to miss: that rituals are not only statements of meaning but also transactions with consequences for who can rely on whom, and that their cost is part of how they work rather than an unfortunate by-product. But it is incomplete in at least three ways. First, it explains why a ritual should be costly, not why it should take the particular form it does. Any sufficiently burdensome act would serve as a signal, yet communities care intensely about the precise words, gestures and times of their observances, and would not accept a different hardship of equal cost as a substitute. Second, many of the most demanding practices are imposed on children long before they could choose them. A rite performed on an infant may say something about the commitment of the parents, but it cannot be a signal of the child's own.

The third gap is the most important. The signalling theory treats ritual as a message addressed to observers, as though the performer were already fully committed and merely needed to prove it. Yet people who undertake demanding practices commonly report that the practice changes them: that the fast or the vigil produces the conviction it was supposed to display. If that is so, cost does not only reveal commitment but helps to manufacture it. A person who has sacrificed much for a group has reasons to value it that did not exist before the sacrifice, and the observer who reads his devotion as a signal is reading something that the ritual itself helped to create.

None of these objections overturns the signalling account. They suggest instead that it describes one function among several, the one most visible to an outsider calculating whom to trust. A fuller theory would ask what ritual does to the people who perform it as well as what it tells the people who watch, and would treat the form of a rite as something that matters in its own right rather than as an arbitrary carrier of cost.

CAT-VARC-P03-024-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ

  1. (A)committed to the group for a short trial period before full membership.
  2. (B)attached to the group through a name inherited from one's family.
  3. (C)belonging to the group in name while lacking any real commitment to it.
  4. (D)connected to the group informally, without ever having formally joined it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The sentence contrasts someone willing to bear years of hardship with someone 'only nominally attached'; the preceding sentences describe people who 'take the benefits of membership without bearing its burdens'. Nominal attachment is membership in name only.

Why not (A): No trial period is mentioned; the phrase describes the kind of member that demanding rituals screen out.

Why not (B): Nothing in paragraph 2 concerns family names; the contrast is between real and merely apparent commitment.

Why not (D): This reverses the sense: the free rider is formally a member who takes the 'benefits of membership', but is not committed.

Common misconception: Reading 'nominally' literally as having to do with names.

Difficulty: The phrase is explained by the free-rider description just before it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-024-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that, on the signalling account, a group that lowers its demands may lose members because
  1. (A)the members who remain must take on a larger share of the cost of the rituals that are left.
  2. (B)outsiders conclude from the change that the group has quietly abandoned its central doctrines.

CAT-VARC-P03-025 · Work, class and status · passage Easy · 431 words · 4 questions

When a job that was once filled by school leavers begins to advertise for graduates, the usual explanation is that the work has become more demanding. Machines have taken over the routine tasks, the story goes, and what remains calls for the analytical habits that only a university can supply. There is something to this. Some occupations really have changed beyond recognition, and an office clerk today handles systems that would have baffled a clerk of fifty years ago. But the explanation is offered far more often than it is checked, and when one looks at what people in many of these jobs actually do, the change in the work is often modest beside the change in the paperwork required to get it.

A more economical account treats the degree less as a certificate of skill than as a ticket in a queue. When few people held degrees, an employer who asked for one was selecting a small group who were, on average, more persistent and better connected than the rest. As degrees became common, the same requirement no longer separated anyone, and employers who wanted to keep selecting raised the bar again, asking for a master's where a bachelor's had once sufficed. Each employer is behaving sensibly; it is merely using the cheapest filter available. Yet the sum of these sensible choices resembles a crowd at a parade in which everyone stands on tiptoe: no one sees better than before, and everyone is more tired.

The costs of this arrangement are not evenly spread. Students from families that can afford several years of fees and forgone earnings keep pace with the rising bar; those who cannot are shut out of work they could perfectly well perform. A requirement introduced in the name of competence thus ends up rewarding the capacity to pay for time. Meanwhile the graduates themselves often discover that much of what they studied is barely used in the job, and that what the employer valued was the signal rather than the substance.

None of this is an argument against education, which has goods that no labour market can measure. It is an argument against confusing two things that the word 'qualification' runs together: what a person can do, and how long a person has been able to stay in school. Some employers have begun to test applicants directly on the tasks the job involves. Where this is done, an awkward question comes into view, namely whether the degree requirement it replaces was ever a guarantee of quality or merely a habit that nobody had thought to examine.

CAT-VARC-P03-025-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The image of a crowd at a parade in which 'everyone stands on tiptoe' (paragraph 2) serves to
  1. (A)suggest that graduates are worn out by the number of years they have spent studying.
  2. (B)show that employers have quietly agreed among themselves to keep raising the qualifications they demand from applicants.
  3. (C)demonstrate that a degree gives an individual employer no useful information about applicants.
  4. (D)illustrate how choices that make sense for each employer combine into an outcome that helps nobody.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The image follows directly on 'Each employer is behaving sensibly' and 'the sum of these sensible choices'. When everyone stands on tiptoe, each person's move makes sense, but 'no one sees better than before, and everyone is more tired': raising the bar for all buys no advantage for anyone and only adds cost.

Why not (A): This reads 'more tired' literally; the image is about the collective result of everyone raising the bar, not about the fatigue of students.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says 'Each employer is behaving sensibly' on its own account; there is no agreement or collusion among employers anywhere in the passage.

Why not (C): The passage says the degree is 'the cheapest filter available' and once selected a more persistent group, so it does carry some information; the image concerns the collective result.

Common misconception: Taking the image literally, or reading collusion into what the passage presents as separate sensible choices.

Difficulty: The sentence introducing the image states its point outright. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-025-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ

Answer: (A)

CAT-VARC-P03-025-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely agree with which of the following?
  1. (A)Governments should prohibit employers from asking applicants for degrees unless the job needs specialist knowledge.
  2. (B)Most jobs now advertised for graduates involve exactly the same tasks that they involved fifty years ago, despite the new paperwork.
  3. (C)A rise in the education a job demands does not by itself show that the job has become harder.
  4. (D)The main value of a university education lies in the signal of persistence it sends to employers.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1 says the explanation that work has become more demanding 'is offered far more often than it is checked', and paragraph 2 offers a rival account in which requirements rise because degrees stop separating applicants. So a rising requirement does not by itself show the work is harder.

Why not (A): The passage proposes no regulation; paragraph 4 mentions employers who test applicants directly, but as a development that raises a question, not as a law the author recommends.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 concedes that 'Some occupations really have changed beyond recognition' and says only that the change in many jobs is 'often modest', which is far weaker than saying most jobs are exactly the same.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says education 'has goods that no labour market can measure', so the author does not reduce its value to a labour-market signal.

Common misconception: Turning the author's 'often modest' into 'no change at all', or crediting the author with a prescription.

Difficulty: Needs the author's hedged position across paragraphs 1 and 2 and rejection of two plausible overstatements. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-026 · Work, class and status · passage Easy · 404 words · 4 questions

We tend to think of the weekend as a natural feature of the calendar, as if two days of rest had always followed five of work. In fact the arrangement is fairly recent, and its history suggests that free time is not simply the absence of work but something that has to be made, defended and shaped. Before the factory, many artisans worked in bursts, labouring late into the night to finish an order and then taking a day or two off whenever it suited them; in parts of England the habit of stretching Sunday's rest into Monday was common enough to be given a mock saint's name, 'Saint Monday'.

Factory production could not tolerate this irregularity. Machines were expensive and had to run whenever the hands were present, and an employer could not plan production around workers who might drift in on Tuesday, so the long struggle over hours was therefore also a struggle over the shape of time. Workers wanted fewer hours; employers wanted predictable ones. The compromise that eventually emerged, first a half-day on Saturday and later the whole of it, gave each side part of what it sought. Workers won rest that was guaranteed rather than snatched, and employers won a workforce that turned up, sober, on Monday morning.

It would be wrong to see the outcome merely as a defeat dressed up as a victory. For most working people, guaranteed free time was a real gain, and it was won against resistance. But the weekend also changed what free time was. Leisure that had once been taken at the worker's discretion became a fixed block, the same for everyone, and a block that was the same for everyone could be sold to. Football matches, excursion trains and seaside resorts arranged themselves around Saturday afternoon and Sunday, and leisure came increasingly to mean something one went out and paid for at a scheduled time.

The lesson is not that the old irregular rhythm was better; it was often tied to poverty, to insecurity and to binges of overwork that left people exhausted. It is that arguments about working hours are never only about quantity. When people today argue over flexible schedules or a four-day week, they are reopening a question that the industrial age settled in one particular way: not merely how much time should be free, but who decides when it falls and what it is for.

In paragraph 3, the remark that 'a block that was the same for everyone could be sold to' most nearly means that
  1. (A)leisure became so expensive that most working families could no longer afford to enjoy the free time they had won.
  2. (B)employers could buy back their workers' Saturdays by offering them extra pay for working at the weekend.
  3. (C)workers were now free to sell their weekend hours to whichever employer offered them the best terms.
  4. (D)free time falling at the same hours for everyone became a market that businesses could plan to serve.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The phrase is explained by the next sentence: football matches, excursion trains and seaside resorts 'arranged themselves around Saturday afternoon and Sunday'. Because everyone was free at the same time, businesses could sell leisure to that shared block.

Why not (A): The passage says leisure came to be paid for, not that it became unaffordable; it calls guaranteed free time 'a real gain' for most working people.

Why not (B): The passage never mentions overtime; the sentence that follows lists football matches, excursion trains and resorts, which sell leisure to workers rather than buy their time.

Why not (C): The sellers in paragraph 3 are leisure businesses, and what changes is that leisure becomes 'something one went out and paid for'; workers are the buyers, not the sellers.

Common misconception: Reading 'sold' as a sale of labour time rather than a sale of leisure to workers.

Difficulty: The following sentence glosses the phrase directly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-027 · Work, class and status · passage Medium · 452 words · 4 questions

In the large households of the nineteenth century, the best servant was the one who was least noticed. Grand houses were built with back staircases so that maids carrying coal or slops need never meet the family on the main stairs, and bells rang in a basement corridor so that a summons could be issued without anyone having to see who answered it. In some houses servants were expected to turn aside, even to face the wall, when a member of the family passed. It is tempting to read all this as snobbery, a taste for keeping inferiors out of sight, but snobbery explains less than it seems to. The invisibility of servants was not a by-product of the genteel household; it was one of the things the household was for.

What a gentleman's house displayed, above all, was ease: meals that appeared, fires that were lit, linen that was clean, all without visible effort. The ease was manufactured by a great deal of labour, and the labour had to be hidden for the ease to be believed. A servant glimpsed scrubbing a step was a reminder that comfort had a cost and a source; a servant unseen allowed the family to experience comfort as a natural condition of their kind of life. In this sense the architecture of concealment did not merely reflect a social hierarchy but helped to produce one, by allowing one class to appear exempt from necessity.

Large-scale domestic service of this kind has largely disappeared, and it is easy to assume that its logic went with it. Yet much of the modern service economy seems designed to recreate the same experience by other means. Food arrives at the door from a kitchen the customer never sees, carried by a courier who is a first name on a screen. Offices are cleaned at night, after the staff have gone home. Apps promise that groceries, laundry and repairs will simply happen, and their designers speak, revealingly, of removing friction, as though the friction in question were not, very often, the encounter with another person who is doing the work.

The comparison must not be pressed too far. The modern courier or cleaner is not bound to a single household, keeps hours that end, and has legal protections that a Victorian scullery maid lacked; to call such workers servants is to erase real gains. But the aspiration that the old houses expressed in brick and bells, the wish to receive service without meeting the server, has proved remarkably durable. What has changed is less the wish than the means of satisfying it, and the means are now available to far more people than ever owned a back staircase.

CAT-VARC-P03-028 · Work, class and status · passage Medium · 420 words · 4 questions

The idea that some jobs require workers to manage their own feelings, and not merely their hands or their minds, has travelled a long way from its origins in the study of flight attendants and debt collectors. It now turns up in discussions of nursing, teaching, retail and call centres, and even of the unpaid work of keeping a family cheerful. This expansion has drawn a predictable objection: if every job involves some management of emotion, the critics say, then the concept marks out nothing in particular. A surgeon must stay calm, a lawyer must appear confident, and a builder who loses his temper with clients will not keep them; are these, too, emotional labourers?

The objection is fair against the loose use of the term, but it misses what the original idea was pointing at. The distinction was never between jobs that involve feeling and jobs that do not. It was between feeling that a worker manages according to her own judgement and feeling whose display is specified, monitored and sold by someone else. The surgeon's calm serves the operation and is left to her; nobody scripts it. The call-centre agent, by contrast, may be told how often to use the customer's name, scored on the warmth of her voice, and disciplined for a sigh. The emotion is part of the product, and the employer, not the worker, sets its terms.

Seen this way, the concept is narrower than its popular use suggests, but also sharper. It directs attention not to the presence of emotion at work but to its ownership, and it suggests where the cost of such work lies. Most people can smile at a stranger without harm; the difficulty arises when the smile must be produced on demand, for hours, regardless of how the worker is treated, and when failing to produce it is a disciplinary matter. One would expect work of this kind to produce a fatigue unlike ordinary tiredness, and perhaps an uncertainty about which friendly feelings are one's own.

None of this implies that friendliness in service is a sham, or that customers who enjoy it are dupes. Many workers take pride in easing a stranger's day, and a scripted greeting can be delivered with real goodwill. The point is that goodwill freely given and goodwill required by contract are different things, even when they look identical from the other side of the counter, and a concept that cannot tell them apart has lost the very thing that made it worth having.

Common misconception: Equating ownership with sincerity, or with the amount of emotion involved.

CAT-VARC-P03-029 · Work, class and status · passage Medium · 422 words · 4 questions

For a long time the sociology of taste worked with a simple map. At the top of the social order sat a refined culture of opera, poetry and the old masters, at the bottom sat popular entertainments, and a person's place in society could be read, with fair accuracy, from the position of their preferences on this ladder. Elites, on this picture, distinguished themselves by what they refused. Liking the wrong music was not merely a private failing but a mark of origin.

Surveys of cultural consumption in a number of wealthy countries have since complicated the map. The most educated and affluent groups, it turns out, are no longer best described as snobs who like only high culture. They are more often omnivores: they go to the opera and to the cinema, read literary fiction and crime novels, and are happy to discuss both a string quartet and a pop album. Some observers have greeted this as the end of cultural hierarchy, a sign that the old boundaries between high and low have dissolved and that taste has become a matter of individual preference rather than of class.

That conclusion is premature. Breadth of taste is itself unevenly distributed, because it requires money, time, travel and, above all, the confidence to move between worlds without feeling out of place. The person who can talk knowledgeably about both a symphony and a street-food stall has accumulated a cultural range that is far harder to acquire than a liking for either alone. Moreover, omnivores rarely consume popular forms in the same way as their usual audiences. They tend to approach them with a knowing, curatorial attitude, prizing a song for its place in the history of a genre or enjoying a film with a touch of irony, and this manner of liking can mark distance as effectively as refusal once did.

Nor is the omnivore's appetite unlimited. Research on what people say they dislike has suggested that the forms most often rejected by the highly educated tend to be those most associated with the least educated, so that the boundary has moved rather than vanished. None of this means that the loosening of old hierarchies is an illusion; it is a genuine change that the opera house must now compete for prestige with a well-chosen food truck. But distinction has not disappeared with the decline of snobbery. It has learned to express itself through openness, and openness is harder to criticise than exclusion because it looks so much like a virtue.

  3. (C)taste was largely a matter of individual preference, with only a loose and unreliable connection to social class.
  4. (D)a person's social standing could be read from where their preferences fell on a ladder from refined to popular culture.

Paragraph 1: 'a person's place in society could be read, with fair accuracy, from the position of their preferences on this ladder', a ladder running from opera, poetry and the old masters down to popular entertainments.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says elites 'distinguished themselves by what they refused', not by how much they spent.

CAT-VARC-P03-029-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author's discussion of the 'knowing, curatorial attitude' (paragraph 3) suggests that
  1. (A)omnivores secretly dislike the popular forms they claim to enjoy and consume them only in order to appear open-minded.
  2. (B)the manner in which something is liked matters more than breadth of taste in every form of social distinction.
  3. (C)two people may enjoy the same popular film while their way of enjoying it marks quite different social positions.
  4. (D)popular culture is transformed into high culture once educated audiences begin to adopt it and write about it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says omnivores 'rarely consume popular forms in the same way as their usual audiences' and that 'this manner of liking can mark distance'. So the same object can be enjoyed by two audiences whose enjoyment signals different positions.

Why not (B): The author offers manner as one marker beside breadth and selective dislike, and never ranks them, let alone for every form of distinction.

Why not (D): The passage says nothing about popular forms changing their status; it is the manner of consumption, not the form, that marks distance.

Common misconception: Turning a claim about manner of enjoyment into insincerity, or into a ranking of markers.

Paragraph 4 says the forms 'most often rejected by the highly educated tend to be those most associated with the least educated', so that 'the boundary has moved rather than vanished'. A self-described omnivore who excludes the one genre of the least schooled fits this exactly.

Why not (B): This is distinction by refusal of the old kind described in paragraph 1, not the moved boundary of the omnivore.

CAT-VARC-P03-030 · Work, class and status · passage Difficult · 466 words · 4 questions

Two accounts of the professions have long competed for our assent, and each is right about something. The first, which professionals themselves favour, holds that a profession is defined by esoteric knowledge put at the service of clients who cannot judge it. Because a patient cannot assess a diagnosis or a litigant a legal argument, the state licenses practitioners, and the licence is a guarantee of competence that protects the public from charlatans. The second account, favoured by economists of a sceptical turn, treats the same licence as a barrier to entry: by limiting the number who may practise, a profession raises its fees, and its talk of public protection is simply the language in which a cartel describes itself.

What the two accounts share is more interesting than what divides them. Both look at one profession at a time, as if each stood alone facing its clients, and both assume that the work a profession does is simply given: there are diseases, so there are doctors; there are disputes, so there are lawyers. Yet the boundaries of professional work are anything but given. Whether persistent unhappiness is a medical condition, a psychological difficulty, a spiritual crisis or a social problem is not settled by the nature of unhappiness. It is settled, provisionally and repeatedly, by contests among psychiatrists, psychologists, clergy and social workers, each of whom claims that the problem properly belongs to it.

Seen from this angle, a profession is less a guild guarding a fixed territory than one party in a system of rival claims, and its history is a history of conquests and retreats. Licensing remains important, but as a weapon in a jurisdictional struggle rather than as either a certificate or a mere device for fixing prices. Knowledge, too, matters in a different way. What wins a contested problem is often not superior skill in treating it but a more abstract body of theory, one that allows a profession to redefine the problem in its own terms and so to argue that its rivals were treating the symptoms rather than the thing itself. A profession whose knowledge is purely practical can be displaced by one that can explain why that practice works.

This does not mean that competence is irrelevant or that the cartel theorist is merely cynical. Clients are protected by licensing, and fees are raised by it. But neither effect explains why some professions grow while others shrink, or why a body of practitioners who are perfectly good at their work can find that work quietly reassigned to others. The fate of a profession, on this view, depends less on how well it does what it does than on its ability to persuade others, including the state and the public, that the problems of the moment are its problems.

CAT-VARC-P03-030-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The example of persistent unhappiness in paragraph 2 serves mainly to
  1. (A)suggest that psychiatrists have been steadily losing ground to clergy and social workers in the treatment of unhappiness.
  2. (B)demonstrate that licensing has failed to protect the public from practitioners who are not properly qualified to help.
  3. (C)illustrate that some human problems are too complex for any single profession to treat adequately on its own.
  4. (D)show that the profession to which a problem belongs is decided by rivalry among professions rather than by the problem itself.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 says the boundaries of professional work 'are anything but given'. Unhappiness could be medical, psychological, spiritual or social, and which it is 'is not settled by the nature of unhappiness' but 'by contests' among professions. The example undermines the shared assumption that a profession's work is simply given.

Why not (A): The passage names the rival claimants but reports no winner or trend; it says only that the question is settled 'provisionally and repeatedly'.

Why not (B): Licensing is not discussed in paragraph 2, and the example concerns which profession owns a problem, not the qualifications of practitioners.

Why not (C): The point is not that the problem exceeds any one profession's ability but that its classification 'is not settled by the nature of unhappiness'.

Common misconception: Reading the example as a report on who is winning, rather than as a counter to the assumption that work is given.

Difficulty: The function depends on the preceding claim about what the two accounts share. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-030-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that the author regards the two accounts in paragraph 1 as
  1. (A)incompatible with each other, so that anyone who accepts one of them is bound to reject the other entirely.
  2. (B)each capturing a real effect of licensing, while sharing an assumption that prevents either from explaining professional change.
  3. (C)correct when taken together, since in combination they give a complete account of how professions rise and fall.
  4. (D)equally mistaken, since licensing in fact neither protects clients from charlatans nor raises the fees that professionals charge.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 says 'each is right about something', and paragraph 4 confirms that licensing both protects clients and raises fees. But paragraph 2 says both 'look at one profession at a time' and treat its work as 'simply given', and paragraph 4 adds that neither explains growth and decline.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 accepts the effect each account describes at once, so the author does not treat them as mutually exclusive.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'neither effect explains why some professions grow while others shrink', so together they remain incomplete.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says 'Clients are protected by licensing, and fees are raised by it', so the author grants both effects.

CAT-VARC-P03-030-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim about how knowledge matters in contests over problems (paragraph 3)?
  1. (A)Practitioners in a profession that lost a problem to a rival were later found to have been entirely competent at treating it.
  2. (B)After one profession won control of a problem, licensing rules were rewritten so as to bar its rivals from treating that problem.

Paragraph 3 claims that 'What wins a contested problem is often not superior skill in treating it but a more abstract body of theory'. A broad pattern in which practical success, regardless of rivals' theories, usually decided the contest directly contradicts this.

Why not (B): This fits the author's description of licensing as 'a weapon in a jurisdictional struggle', and says nothing against the role of theory.

Why not (D): This is consistent with the author's view: theory won the problem, and the loss fits a history 'of conquests and retreats'.

CAT-VARC-P03-030-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely agree with which of the following?
  1. (A)The state's part in professional life is confined to certifying the competence of individual practitioners through licences.
  2. (B)A body of practitioners could become more skilled over a decade and still end it with a narrower field of work.
  3. (C)The size of a profession at a given moment is a reliable measure of how well it serves the clients who rely on it.
  4. (D)A profession armed with abstract theory will always defeat a rival whose knowledge is purely practical in a contest over a problem.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says practitioners 'perfectly good at their work can find that work quietly reassigned', because fate depends 'less on how well it does what it does' than on claiming problems. Growing skill is therefore compatible with a shrinking jurisdiction.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 treats licensing as 'a weapon in a jurisdictional struggle', and paragraph 4 names the state among those a profession must persuade.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says a profession's fate 'depends less on how well it does what it does' than on its persuasiveness, so size does not track quality of service.

CAT-VARC-P03-031 · Work, class and status · passage Medium · 596 words · 5 questions

The phrase 'conspicuous consumption', coined by Thorstein Veblen in the 1890s, named a habit of the wealthy of his day: the purchase of goods whose chief use was to show that one could afford them. The mansion, the carriage and the idle household were, on this account, less comforts than advertisements. The idea proved so useful that it outlived the society that prompted it, and it still shapes how we picture status spending: the logo on the handbag, the oversized car, the watch that costs more than a year's rent.

The picture now misleads more than it reveals, at least at the top. As mass production and global supply chains have made once-rare goods cheap, the handbag and the car have become available, in passable imitations, to almost anyone with a credit card. Display of this kind has lost much of its power to separate, and many of the most advantaged households have turned away from it. Their spending flows instead into things that are hard to see and harder to copy: private tuition and a house in the right school catchment, retirement savings, organic groceries, exercise classes, domestic help that frees time for reading, and clothing whose quality is recognised only by those who know what to look for. The signal, where there is one, is addressed to a narrow audience of peers rather than to the street. The pattern has come to be called inconspicuous consumption.

It would be easy to treat the change as an improvement in taste, a retreat from vulgar display into quieter and more sensible habits. In part it is. Much of this spending yields real benefits in health, in learning and in security, and few would wish the rich to return to buying gold-plated taps. But the shift has two consequences that the language of good taste conceals. The first is that inconspicuous goods are more effective at reproducing advantage than conspicuous ones ever were. A diamond can be sold; a childhood spent in good schools, with books at home and parents who have time, becomes part of a person and can neither be taken away nor easily acquired later. Spending of this kind does not merely display a position; it passes it on.

The second consequence is moral. Conspicuous consumption invited criticism precisely because it was conspicuous. The flashy car was an obvious target for the satirist and the moralist, and its owner could be accused of vanity. Inconspicuous consumption is much harder to criticise, because it presents itself not as display but as virtue: one is not showing off, one is merely eating well, looking after one's health and investing in one's children. The vocabulary that surrounds it, of wellness, sensible choices and doing the best for one's family, offers the satirist nothing obviously vulgar to mock. Those who cannot afford to do the same are not envied so much as quietly judged, as though their diets and their children's results were failures of character rather than of income.

None of this is an accusation of bad faith. It does not require us to think the affluent hypocritical, or the parent paying for tuition secretly bent on buying status; most such choices are made sincerely, for the reasons people give. The point is structural rather than personal. A society in which advantage is transmitted through the visible accumulation of objects at least makes its inequalities legible. One in which advantage flows through habits, schooling and time has made them harder to see, and inequalities that are hard to see are also harder to argue about.

CAT-VARC-P03-032 · Work, class and status · passage Difficult · 576 words · 5 questions

Few political promises are as widely shared as the promise of social mobility. Parties that agree on little else agree that a child's prospects should not be fixed by the circumstances of her birth, and the claim that mobility has stalled is one of the most damaging charges that can be laid against a government. Yet the word covers two quite different things, and much public argument about it goes astray because the two are run together.

The first is absolute mobility: the share of people who end up better off than their parents, whether by income or by the kind of work they do. The second is relative mobility: the extent to which a person's position in the distribution, as opposed to their standard of living, differs from their parents' position. The two can move independently. In a period of rapid growth and expanding professional employment, most children may outearn their parents and many may enter occupations their parents never held, even if the chance of a child born near the bottom reaching the top, compared with that of a child born near the top, has not changed at all. Everyone rides the escalator up, but the order of people on it stays the same.

This distinction explains a puzzle. When growth slows and the expansion of professional jobs levels off, absolute mobility falls, and people who do no better than their parents feel, reasonably enough, that something has closed. They often conclude that society has become less fair. Imagine a town where a mill employed one generation and new offices employed the next. The children did better than their parents without anyone being pushed aside, and the town experienced this as openness. When the offices stop hiring, the grandchildren find that the climb has ended. Yet on the measures that track their position against the children of the office managers, the chances may be much as they were: what has disappeared is not openness but the rising tide that once let a whole generation move up without anyone having to move down. The sense of closure is real; its diagnosis is frequently mistaken.

The distinction also exposes an awkwardness that politicians prefer to leave unspoken. Absolute mobility can, in principle, be enjoyed by everyone at once. Relative mobility cannot. If positions are ranked, then for one family to climb another must fall, and a serious commitment to relative mobility is therefore a commitment to downward mobility for some children of the comfortable. It is not surprising that the comfortable resist it, often without any sense of doing so, through the ordinary devices of private tuition, a well-placed word, or the purchase of a house near the right school. Each of these looks like ordinary care for one's own children; together they amount to a defence of position.

None of this makes one kind of mobility the real one. Absolute mobility answers a question about material progress: are people living better than their parents did? Relative mobility answers a question about fairness: does birth still decide where one ends up? Both questions matter, and neither is easy to answer with confidence, since incomes fluctuate and occupations change their meaning across generations. But a society that wants more of the second kind should be honest that it cannot be had at no one's expense, and a society that mourns the loss of the first should not mistake a slower economy for a more rigid one.

Why not (B): The image says 'the order of people on it stays the same', so no group overtakes another.

Common misconception: Reading the image as a claim about effort or about who rises faster.

CAT-VARC-P03-032-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following would be a case of relative mobility rising while absolute mobility falls, in the passage's sense?
  1. (A)In a booming economy where most children outearn their parents, the order of families in the income ranking remains exactly what it was before.
  2. (B)In a booming economy where nearly all children outearn their parents, the income ranking is substantially reshuffled from one generation to the next.
  3. (C)In a stagnant economy where few children outearn their parents, children of poor families increasingly overtake children of rich families in the income ranking.
  4. (D)In a stagnant economy where few children outearn their parents, children remain in much the same place in the income ranking as their parents held.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Absolute mobility is 'the share of people who end up better off than their parents'; relative mobility concerns changes in 'position in the distribution'. A stagnant economy where few outearn their parents has low absolute mobility, and poor children overtaking rich ones in rank is a rise in relative mobility.

Why not (A): This is the escalator of paragraph 2: absolute mobility is high and relative mobility unchanged, the reverse of what is asked.

Why not (B): Here absolute mobility rises, since nearly all outearn their parents, so it is a case of both kinds rising, not of absolute mobility falling.

Why not (D): Absolute mobility falls here, but relative mobility is unchanged rather than rising; this is the situation described in paragraph 3.

Common misconception: Mixing up the two measures, especially choosing the paragraph 3 case in which relative mobility merely stays the same.

Difficulty: Requires applying both definitions to four cases that differ on two dimensions. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-032-Q4 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best captures the main argument of the passage?
  1. (A)Relative mobility is the only true measure of whether a society is open, while absolute mobility merely reflects how fast its economy is growing.
  2. (B)Since absolute and relative mobility answer different questions and can diverge, running them together leads people to misread slowdowns and to ignore what fairness costs.
  3. (C)Social mobility has not stalled as its critics claim; it has simply become much harder to measure than it was in earlier generations.

Answer: (B)

Why not (A): Paragraph 5 says 'None of this makes one kind of mobility the real one', and that the question absolute mobility answers also matters.

Why not (C): The author accepts that absolute mobility falls when growth slows, and the difficulty of measurement is a passing remark, not the thesis.

Why not (D): This narrows the passage to paragraph 4 and adds a prescription; the author asks for honesty about costs, not for action against families.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that ranks one kind of mobility above the other, which the final paragraph explicitly rejects.

CAT-VARC-P03-032-Q5 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely say that a government promising to raise relative mobility while ensuring that no family moves down the ranking
  1. (A)is promising something that cannot be delivered when positions are ranked, whatever the rate of economic growth.
  2. (B)is making essentially the same promise as a government that pledges to raise the living standards of each new generation.
  3. (C)could keep its promise provided that the economy grew fast enough to raise the incomes of every family at once.
  4. (D)could keep its promise by abolishing private tuition and other devices that comfortable families use to defend their position.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4: 'If positions are ranked, then for one family to climb another must fall', so relative mobility 'cannot' be enjoyed by everyone at once, unlike absolute mobility. Why not (C): Growth raises absolute mobility, which can 'be enjoyed by everyone at once'; it cannot let one family climb the ranking without another falling.

Why not (D): Removing such devices might raise relative mobility, but paragraph 4 says that 'for one family to climb another must fall', so some would still move down.
