CAT-VARC-P03-033 · Consumption and identity · passage Easy · 438 words · 4 questions

Ask a collector why she keeps buying stamps, coins or toy cars long after there is any room left to display them, and the answer is rarely about the objects themselves. More often it concerns a gap: the one issue missing from a run, the single variant that would finish a set. Collecting, on this view, is less a love of things than a love of completion. The individual stamp matters because of the place it occupies in a series, and its value to the collector rises sharply as the series nears its end, often far beyond anything a buyer outside the hobby would think of paying.

Manufacturers learned long ago to exploit this. A cereal box that contains one of twelve plastic figures, a sticker album with a few deliberately scarce pages, a line of trading cards issued in waves: each turns an ordinary purchase into a step towards a whole that the buyer did not know he wanted until he was shown its outline. The empty album is the real product. Once its spaces are printed, every blank becomes a small reproach, and the purchase that fills it brings a satisfaction out of all proportion to what was bought.

It would be easy to conclude that collecting is simply a manufactured compulsion, a weakness that the market has learned to farm. That conclusion goes too far. Many of the most devoted collectors pursue series that no company ever designed: the railway timetables of a vanished line, the matchboxes of hotels that have since closed, the scattered editions of a minor poet. Here it is the collector who defines the set, deciding what counts as belonging to it and therefore what counts as missing. Such collecting is a way of ordering the world, of drawing a boundary around a scattered field and making it intelligible. Many museums began, in part, as exactly this kind of private project.

The difference between the two kinds of collecting is not the passion involved, which can be equally intense, but who holds the map. When the series is invented by a seller, completion is a target set by someone else, and the finish line is often moved just as it comes into view, with a new wave or a special edition. When the collector draws the map, completion is a judgement she makes for herself, and the pleasure lies as much in the defining as in the finding. The danger of the commercial version is not that it creates desire, since all collecting does that, but that it quietly takes the act of definition away from the person doing the collecting.

CAT-VARC-P03-033-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)argue that collectors value the objects they gather for their beauty and rarity rather than for the places they fill in a set, whoever happens to have designed that set.
  2. (B)trace how private collections of timetables, matchboxes and editions gradually developed into the public museums of today, and to praise the collectors who began them.
  3. (C)show that collecting is a compulsion created by manufacturers, who profit by persuading buyers to complete sets that they never wanted until the empty spaces were shown to them.
  4. (D)distinguish collecting in which the collector defines the series from collecting in which a seller does, and to show that the latter's harm lies in removing that power.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 identifies the love of completion, paragraph 2 shows sellers exploiting it, paragraph 3 rejects the conclusion that collecting is therefore a manufactured compulsion, and paragraph 4 states that the real difference is 'who holds the map', the danger being that commercial series take 'the act of definition' away from the collector.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says the opposite: collecting is 'less a love of things than a love of completion', and a stamp matters for 'the place it occupies in a series'.

Why not (B): Museums appear in a single sentence of paragraph 3 as a supporting aside; the passage gives no history of their development, so the option is far too narrow.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 calls this conclusion one that 'goes too far' and offers collectors who pursue series 'that no company ever designed', so the option states a view the author rejects.

Common misconception: Taking the description of manufacturers in paragraph 2 for the thesis, and missing the concession in paragraph 3 that turns the argument.

Difficulty: The tempting option restates paragraph 2 and the view the author rejects; the correct answer requires seeing the turn in paragraph 3 and the conclusion in paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-034 · Consumption and identity · passage Easy · 402 words · 4 questions

A curious feature of the market for retro goods is how many of their buyers are too young to remember the originals. Record players styled like those of the nineteen-sixties, cameras that imitate the look of early film models, soft drinks reissued in the bottles of half a century ago: these sell briskly to people for whom the period they evoke is something seen in old films rather than lived. Whatever such buyers are nostalgic for, it cannot be their own past, and any explanation of the trade has to begin from that awkward fact.

The usual explanation is that they are being fooled, that advertisers have invented a golden age and sold it back to a generation with no means of checking. There is something in this, but it misreads what the buyer is after. Nostalgia has always been less a memory than a mood, a way of expressing dissatisfaction with the present by furnishing it with an imagined contrast against which its deficiencies become visible. The retro object does not claim to restore the past; it offers a small, ownable piece of a world that seems slower, sturdier and easier to understand than the one its owner actually lives in. That such a world never quite existed matters less than the fact that the feeling it answers is real.

What is striking is how carefully the past is edited in the process. The reissued record player has a socket for a phone; the vintage-look camera stores its pictures on a memory card and adds the scratches digitally. The inconveniences that made the originals what they were, the waiting, the expense, the fragility, are removed, and only the surface is kept. Retro design is in this sense the opposite of preservation: it keeps the look of an earlier time while discarding the conditions that gave the look its meaning.

None of this need be a cause for scorn. Some revived products are genuinely better made than the disposable goods they replaced, and a taste for the durable, in an economy organised around rapid obsolescence, is not foolish. But it is worth being clear about what is being bought. The buyer of a retro radio is not recovering a lost way of life; he is purchasing a token of his unease with his own, and the market is happy to supply as many such tokens as that unease will bear.

CAT-VARC-P03-035 · Consumption and identity · passage Medium · 405 words · 4 questions

Few words are used more freely by restaurant reviewers than 'authentic', and few are applied more selectively. A dish is praised as authentic when it is served in a cramped room, by a cook who speaks the diner's language haltingly, at a price that suggests nobody is getting rich. The same dish, served with tablecloths and a wine list, is suspected of having been tamed. Authenticity, in this usage, is less a property of the food than of the scene around it, and the scene is one that the visiting diner has come to consume.

Consider a hypothetical family that opens a restaurant serving the cooking of its home region in a city several thousand kilometres away. From the start, the cooks make changes. Some ingredients cannot be found and are replaced with local ones; some dishes are too laborious for a kitchen that must turn out fifty plates a night; others are sweetened or softened because the first customers sent them back. Within a generation the menu has become something that exists nowhere else: neither the food of the home region nor the food of the new city, but the food of the people who moved between them.

Purists treat this history as a decline, and they reward the restaurants that seem to have resisted it, the ones with a second menu in the home language that outsiders must know to ask for. Yet even that second menu has passed through the same kitchen, the same suppliers and the same compromises. To call it the real food and the first menu a betrayal is to take one stage of a continuous adaptation and freeze it as the original. More troubling, the judgement is usually made by people with no stake in the tradition, who are in effect asking immigrant cooks to perform their own foreignness for an audience's satisfaction.

None of this means that every adaptation is innocent. Cooks under commercial pressure sometimes strip a cuisine of everything difficult until it is recognisable only by name, and something real is lost when that happens. But the loss is better described as a narrowing of what the cooks themselves are free to make than as a departure from a fixed original. The more useful question to ask of a restaurant is not whether its food matches some imagined source, but whether the people cooking it have the room to cook what they think is good.

CAT-VARC-P03-036 · Consumption and identity · passage Medium · 403 words · 4 questions

An old explanation of fashion holds that it trickles down. Those at the top of a society adopt a new style to mark themselves off; those just below copy it, hoping to borrow some of the prestige; and once the style has spread far enough to lose its power to distinguish, the people at the top abandon it for something else. Fashion, on this account, is a chase that can never end, because the very success of imitation destroys the value of what is imitated.

The obvious objection is that much of what is worn today did not begin at the top. Jeans were work clothes; the hooded sweatshirt was athletic gear; a great many styles that now appear on expensive runways were first worn by the young, the poor or the marginal, and were adopted by designers only later. If fashion so often bubbles up rather than trickling down, the old theory seems simply mistaken about the direction in which influence travels, and with it, one might suppose, about everything that followed from that direction.

But direction was never the heart of the theory. Its real claim was that fashion is driven by a double motive, the wish to belong and the wish to stand apart, and that the two cannot both be satisfied for long. That claim survives the reversal untouched. When a luxury house borrows the clothing of a street subculture, it is not abandoning distinction but finding a new source of it: the style carries a borrowed credibility that a conventional luxury garment lacks, and wearing it signals a knowingness that cannot be acquired simply by paying more. The members of the subculture, for their part, frequently drop the style once it appears in expensive shops, for precisely the reason the old theory would predict.

What has changed is the speed. When copies of a new look can reach inexpensive shops within weeks, visible novelty becomes a weak marker of status, since it is available to almost anyone almost at once. Increasingly, those who most wish to stand apart do so not by wearing what is new but by having had it first: by access to small releases that sell out in minutes, by acquaintance with designers before they are celebrated, by knowing what is coming rather than displaying what has arrived. The chase continues; it has simply moved to ground where the pursuers find it harder to follow.

CAT-VARC-P03-036-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the trickle-down account described in the passage, those at the top abandon a style when
  1. (A)designers begin borrowing it from the clothing of a street subculture.
  2. (B)cheap copies of it begin to reach shops within a few weeks of its appearance.
  3. (C)it has spread so widely that it no longer sets them apart from others.
  4. (D)the people just below them stop copying it and turn to something new.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 1: 'once the style has spread far enough to lose its power to distinguish, the people at the top abandon it for something else'.

Why not (A): Borrowing from subcultures belongs to the objection and reply in paragraphs 2 and 3, not to the trickle-down account of paragraph 1.

Why not (B): The speed of copying is the change described in paragraph 4, a later development, not part of the old account.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 says the opposite: it is the success of the copying, not its end, that makes the style lose its value.

Common misconception: Importing the later material on subcultures or speed into the description of the old theory.

Difficulty: Located in one sentence of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-037 · Consumption and identity · passage Medium · 407 words · 4 questions

When a global fast-food chain enters a new country, the first thing commentators examine is its menu. If the chain serves the same burger it serves at home, critics see cultural flattening, the erosion of local tastes by a single corporate palate. If it introduces a spiced vegetable patty or a rice dish for the local market, its defenders announce that globalisation, far from erasing difference, accommodates it. Both sides agree, without ever saying so, that the menu is where the question of cultural influence will be settled.

That assumption deserves more scrutiny than it usually receives. A menu is the part of a chain that is easiest to change and, for that very reason, the part that tells us least. What the chain actually exports is a format: a way of organising food, labour and time. The counter at which one orders without a waiter, the fixed price displayed in advance, the promise that the meal will taste the same today as it did last month, the kitchen run like an assembly line by staff who can be trained in days: these travel intact, and they carry assumptions about efficiency and predictability that are far more foreign to many food cultures than any particular ingredient. A spiced patty served through such a system is a local flavour in a global grammar.

Yet it would be wrong to infer that the format simply imposes its meanings wherever it goes, for customers bring meanings of their own. In some places a visit to a chain restaurant has been, at least for a time, a special occasion rather than a convenience: a clean, cool and respectable room in which a family can celebrate a birthday, or two young people can meet without attracting comment. The format was designed for speed, but the customers linger. They take a system built to minimise the time spent at the table and turn it into a place for spending time.

The interesting story, then, has three layers rather than one. The format is global and changes slowly; the meanings attached to it are local and can diverge sharply from what its designers intended; the menu sits between them, adjusted constantly in response to both. To read cultural influence from the menu alone is like judging the spread of a language by the loanwords it has picked up, while ignoring both its grammar and the uses to which its new speakers put it.

CAT-VARC-P03-037-Q1 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In paragraph 2, the phrase 'a local flavour in a global grammar' suggests that
  1. (A)the chain's local dishes are so heavily modified that they no longer taste like anything eaten locally.
  2. (B)local cooks have learned to describe their traditional dishes in the vocabulary of international cuisine.
  3. (C)a locally adapted dish is delivered through a structure that is itself global and carries its own assumptions.
  4. (D)global chains succeed only when they combine local ingredients with recipes perfected in the home country.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The sentence closes a paragraph arguing that the chain exports 'a format', the counter, fixed prices, consistency and the assembly-line kitchen, which carries 'assumptions about efficiency and predictability'. The patty supplies local content; the format is the global structure, the 'grammar', that organises it.

Why not (A): The phrase grants that the flavour is local; the point concerns the system that serves it, not a loss of local taste.

Why not (B): The passage is not about how dishes are described; 'grammar' refers to the format of ordering, pricing and production set out in paragraph 2.

Why not (D): The passage makes no claim about what makes chains succeed, and the word "only" adds a condition found nowhere in the text.

Common misconception: Reading 'grammar' as language about food rather than as a metaphor for the format.

Difficulty: The metaphor must be decoded from the paragraph it concludes. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-038 · Consumption and identity · passage Difficult · 461 words · 4 questions

It has become common to describe the fashion for owning less, the bare room, the small wardrobe of good clothes, the ritual of discarding whatever does not earn its place, as a revolt against consumer society. Its advocates present it as a turn from having to being; its critics, when they bother, dismiss it as one more passing style. Both responses take the movement at its word, as a stance towards consumption. Neither asks what owning little communicates, which is a different question from what it intends.

At the end of the nineteenth century the economist Thorstein Veblen described how the wealthy display their standing through conspicuous consumption, spending visibly on what serves display rather than use. The sparse interior seems at first to invert his picture. Yet emptiness has its own costs, and they are not evenly distributed. A bare floor in a crowded city is a quantity of expensive space left deliberately unfilled. A wardrobe of a dozen garments presupposes that each is durable enough, and the owner's life predictable enough, to require no more. Above all, the confident discarding of objects depends on the assurance that they can be replaced when needed. Households that live close to scarcity keep things, the spare part, the old jar, the coat a size too large, because they cannot count on being able to buy them again; for them, accumulation is a form of insurance. To throw away freely is to display that one has no need of such insurance.

It does not follow that those who embrace minimalism are hypocrites whose real aim is to impress. That objection confuses motive with function. A practice can be sincerely chosen for its own reasons, calm, order, a dislike of waste, and still operate socially as a signal that its practitioners neither chose nor want. Many who pare down their possessions are doing exactly what they say. The point is rather that the meaning of a pattern of consumption is not fixed by the consumer's intention; it is fixed by what the pattern makes legible to others, and what restraint makes legible, in a society where most people cannot afford its preconditions, is security.

The more consequential lesson concerns the hope of opting out. If even refusal can be read as a position within the system of display, then consuming less is not, by itself, an exit from that system; it is a move within it, and often one available only to those already well placed. This is not an argument for consuming more. It is an argument for suspicion towards any claim that a change in private taste can amount to a social critique, since the field of taste has repeatedly proved able to absorb its own refusals and return them as distinctions.

  3. (C)they reject the fashion for owning less as one more passing style of the wealthy.
  4. (D)they have more space at home than wealthier households living in crowded cities.

Common misconception: Attributing to poorer households a view the passage assigns to minimalism's critics.

Difficulty: Stated directly in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-039 · Consumption and identity · passage Medium · 590 words · 5 questions

Marketers like to say that the strongest brands have communities rather than customers. They point to the owners of a particular make of motorcycle who ride together on weekends and have its emblem tattooed on their arms, to the users of a computer maker's products who queue overnight for a new model and argue online with the users of its rivals, and to the enthusiasts who restore and exhibit a discontinued car long after its manufacturer has stopped caring about it. Some commentators go further and call such groups tribes, or even congregations, complete with rituals, myths and heretics, as though buying a product were the first step in a conversion.

The comparison is not empty. Members of these groups share a body of lore, the founding story, the legendary models, the right and wrong ways to use the product, and newcomers learn it much as initiates learn a tradition. They define themselves partly against outsiders, often the owners of a rival brand, and they police their own boundaries, distinguishing the real enthusiast from the person who has merely bought the thing. Most strikingly, they develop a sense of ownership over the brand that is not legal but moral. When a company alters a celebrated design or moves production to another country, its most loyal customers frequently respond not as disappointed consumers but as betrayed members, as though something that belonged to them had been taken without their consent.

Yet the tribal analogy conceals as much as it reveals. Membership of a tribe is not usually something one buys, and leaving it is not usually as easy as buying something else. A brand community, by contrast, is entered through a purchase and can be abandoned at the price of another. More important, the symbols around which it gathers are the legal property of a firm whose interests are not the members' interests. The firm can redesign the emblem, discontinue the product or sell the brand to a competitor, and the members, however devoted, have no formal say in any of it. A tribe whose totem could be sold at auction would be a strange tribe indeed.

It is tempting to resolve the tension by declaring one side the truth: either the community is an illusion manufactured by advertising, or the commercial setting is incidental and the solidarity is all that matters. Neither will do. The solidarity is real; people form friendships, give one another practical help and find meaning in these groups, and much of this happens without the company's involvement or even against its wishes. But the solidarity is organised around something its members do not control, and that fact shapes it. The anger that follows an unpopular redesign is the anger of people discovering, often for the first time, the limits of an ownership they had felt but never possessed.

A brand community, then, is best understood neither as a tribe that happens to shop nor as a market that happens to feel warm, but as a peculiar hybrid: a genuine collective built on borrowed ground. Firms that grasp this tend to treat their communities with caution, consulting them, deferring to them on matters of heritage and sometimes ceding them real influence over decisions. Firms that do not, and that treat the community merely as a marketing asset to be managed, are apt to learn how quickly devotion can turn into grievance, and how little the language of community protects a firm once its members conclude that they have been treated as an audience rather than as partners.

  2. (B)Brand communities involve real solidarity, but because they gather around symbols a firm owns, the tribal comparison is only partly apt.
  3. (C)Brand communities are true tribes in every important respect, and firms that fail to see this lose the loyalty of their best customers.
  4. (D)Firms ought to give the members of their brand communities legal rights over the designs and emblems that the members care about.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 grants what the analogy captures, paragraph 3 says what it hides, paragraph 4 rejects both one-sided resolutions, and paragraph 5 concludes that a brand community is 'a genuine collective built on borrowed ground'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 names this as one of two tempting resolutions and says 'Neither will do', adding that 'The solidarity is real'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the analogy 'conceals as much as it reveals', listing ways in which brand communities differ from tribes.

Why not (D): The passage notes that wise firms consult their communities, but it proposes no legal rights; this adds a prescription.

Common misconception: Choosing one of the two one-sided views that paragraph 4 explicitly rejects.

Difficulty: The correct answer must hold the concession and the critique together; two wrong options keep only one. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-039-Q5 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author's remark that the anger after an unpopular redesign reveals 'the limits of an ownership they had felt but never possessed' (paragraph 4) implies that
  1. (A)members' legal rights over the brand had been violated when the firm altered its design.

  3. (C)members had cared mainly about the product and not about one another all along.
  4. (D)the community had never been more than an illusion, which the redesign finally exposed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The members felt the brand belonged to them, a moral ownership (paragraph 2), but the symbols are 'the legal property of a firm' over which they 'have no formal say' (paragraph 3). The redesign shows the gap between felt and actual ownership.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 calls their ownership 'not legal but moral', and paragraph 3 says they 'have no formal say', so no legal rights were violated.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'people form friendships, give one another practical help', so the remark does not imply that members cared only about the product.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 rejects the view that the community is 'an illusion manufactured by advertising'; the remark concerns limits of ownership, not the unreality of the group.

Common misconception: Treating the anger as proof that the community was unreal, the view paragraph 4 rejects.

Difficulty: Requires combining the moral ownership of paragraph 2 with the legal facts of paragraph 3, while avoiding a rejected view. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P03-040 · Consumption and identity · passage Difficult · 579 words · 5 questions

Tourists, it is often observed, are rarely content with what is shown to them. The monument, the parade, the dance performed on a stage in the hotel courtyard: all of these are understood to be presentations, arranged for visitors, and for that reason they seem to many travellers less real than whatever lies behind them. What the discerning visitor wants is the back region, the kitchen rather than the dining room, the village household rather than the folk show, the place where people are simply living rather than performing. Tourism, on this view, is a search for the unperformed, conducted by people whose very presence makes performance likely.

The difficulty is that the search creates its object, for once visitors will pay to see behind the scenes, behind the scenes becomes a scene. Homestays are furnished to look lived in; working kitchens are tidied and lit for the guided tour; a weaving cooperative arranges its looms so that visitors can watch the ordinary work of the day at a convenient hour. Each of these presents itself as a glimpse of the back, and each is in fact another front, built in the shape of a back. Critics of tourism have made much of this, arguing that the visitor who congratulates himself on having escaped the tourist trail has merely been led down a more expensive branch of it.

The criticism is accurate about the arrangements and, I think, mistaken about the tourists. It assumes that they are dupes who believe they have found the real thing and would be dismayed to learn otherwise. Many are nothing of the kind: asked directly, a good number will say that of course the homestay was prepared for them, that of course the cooperative works differently when no one is watching. What they value is not the illusion of having seen the unstaged but the experience of being admitted, of being received as a guest rather than seated as a spectator, and that experience is not falsified by the knowledge that it was arranged. A theatre audience is not deceived merely because it knows the actors are acting.

If there is a cost in all this, it falls elsewhere. Every back region that is opened to visitors must be replaced by another, further back, where the hosts can live without being watched, and in places where tourism is the main source of income, that retreat can go a long way. The family that rents out its courtyard confines its own life to a back room; the village that stages its festival for visitors holds the version that matters to it in the off season, or not at all. The more successfully a community sells access to its private life, the less private life it has left to sell, and the less it has left to keep for itself.

The usual question about staged authenticity is whether the tourist is being cheated. That question flatters the tourist by placing his experience at the centre of the moral picture, and it is in any case usually answered in his favour, since he generally receives what he knowingly pays for. The harder question concerns the hosts, and it is not whether they are deceiving anyone but how much of their lives they can afford to keep out of view. A tourism that respected its hosts would measure itself not by how much it manages to reveal but by how much it is content to leave alone.

CAT-VARC-P03-041 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Medium · 425 words · 4 questions

Modern moral intuition tends to reserve its highest praise for the gift that expects nothing in return. A present given with an eye to what will come back seems to us slightly tarnished, closer to a trade dressed up in ribbon than to generosity. Anthropologists who have studied exchange in a wide range of societies have long found this intuition puzzling, not because people elsewhere are more calculating, but because in most places the obligation to return is understood as part of what a gift is. To receive is to accept a tie; to fail to reciprocate is not merely rude but a refusal of the relationship the gift proposed.

Consider how carefully the timing and size of a return are managed in many communities. A neighbour who sends back a dish of sweets on the very evening she received one has not discharged a debt so much as declined a friendship; the speed of her reply announces that she wants the account closed. A return that matches the original exactly carries a similar message. What sustains the bond is a return that comes later and differs slightly in value, so that one party is always, for the moment, a little in the other's debt. On this view the imbalance is not a flaw to be corrected but the mechanism by which the relationship is kept running: an account that is never quite settled is an account that remains open.

Seen in this light, the free gift is less an ideal than an anomaly. A gift that truly demanded nothing would create no tie, and a society composed only of such gifts would be a collection of strangers who happened to hand each other things. It is telling that the gifts we most often describe as free, such as anonymous donations, are precisely those designed to prevent any relationship between giver and receiver from forming.

None of this means that the obligation to return is always benign. Where one party can give far more than the other could ever repay, the gift becomes an instrument of domination: the receiver is bound by a debt that cannot be cleared, and gratitude hardens into dependence. Patrons have long understood this, and so have recipients of charity, who sometimes refuse help precisely because they cannot answer it. The lesson, however, is not that we should aspire to gifts without strings. It is that the strings must be ones the receiver is able to hold. A generous act, properly understood, is calibrated to the other person's capacity to reply.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says the quick return means she 'declined a friendship', which is the opposite of valuing it more highly.

Why not (D): The passage says nothing about her means; it interprets the timing as a message that 'she wants the account closed'.

Common misconception: Reading a prompt return as eager goodwill, which is the modern intuition the passage questions.

CAT-VARC-P03-042 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Easy · 413 words · 4 questions

The story is told in so many introductory textbooks that it has come to seem like common sense. Once upon a time, it runs, people traded goods directly: a farmer with grain who wanted shoes had to find a shoemaker who happened to want grain. Because such a coincidence was rare, someone eventually hit on the idea of a commodity everyone would accept, such as shells, salt or metal, and money was born. Credit and debt, in this account, come last, a refinement that becomes possible only once coins are available to measure what is owed.

The difficulty is that the story describes a world no one has found. Anthropologists who went looking for economies in which neighbours regularly exchanged goods by barter have, on the whole, come back empty-handed. What they found instead, in village after village, was something closer to running accounts. A woman who needs a pot from her neighbour simply takes it, and both understand that something will be owed; the debt may be repaid in grain at harvest, in labour at a wedding, or never precisely at all. Among people who expect to keep living beside one another, the problem of the double coincidence of wants scarcely arises, because no one needs the exchange to be completed on the spot.

Barter does occur, but in revealing places. It appears between strangers who do not expect to meet again, between groups who distrust each other, and among people who were once used to money and have been cut off from it, such as prisoners trading cigarettes or townspeople whose currency has collapsed. In each case barter is a response to the absence of trust, not the primitive condition out of which trust and credit later grew.

Defenders of the textbook story sometimes reply that it was never intended as history. It is, they say, a thought experiment showing why a medium of exchange is useful, and its value does not depend on whether any society actually passed through a barter stage. This is a fair point as far as it goes. But the thought experiment is not idle. By placing money first and debt last, it teaches us to see money as a neutral token that merely smooths trade between independent individuals, rather than as a way of recording obligations between people already bound to one another. If the record points the other way, the more natural conclusion is that money began as a method of keeping score.

CAT-VARC-P03-043 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Medium · 414 words · 4 questions

We tend to picture hospitality as the purest form of generosity: a door opened to someone who has no claim on us and nothing to give in return. The picture is not false, but it omits half of what the practice involves. In the many traditions in which welcoming strangers is held to be a sacred duty, the welcome is also a careful exercise of authority. The host decides where the guest will sit, what he will eat, when he will speak and, eventually, when he will leave. To accept hospitality is to accept, for a time, a place inside someone else's order.

This asymmetry is not incidental. A stranger arriving at a settlement is, from the settlement's point of view, an unknown quantity: he may be a trader, a refugee, a spy or a raider. Hospitality converts this uncertain figure into a guest, someone whose position is defined and whose conduct is now governed by rules that the host administers. The rituals that so often accompany a welcome, such as the offering of water, the sharing of salt or bread and the formal exchange of names, can be read as stages in this conversion. The guest who eats at the host's table has implicitly agreed not to harm the household, and the host, in turn, has made himself responsible for the guest's safety against third parties.

It is significant, too, that hospitality is so often limited in time. Many customs specify that a guest may stay a few days before his status must change; after that he becomes a dependant who works, a kinsman by adoption, or simply an unwelcome burden. The welcome is generous precisely because it is temporary. A guest who could stay forever on a guest's terms would gradually unsettle the household's order, and the host would cease to be master of his own house.

Some philosophers have pressed for an ideal of unconditional hospitality, a welcome that asks no questions and sets no limits. As a corrective to the suspicion with which outsiders are often treated, the ideal has force. But taken literally it would dissolve the very relation it seeks to purify. Hospitality requires a host, someone who is at home and who can therefore offer a place; if every condition is removed, there is no longer anyone in a position to welcome. The authority that makes us uneasy is not a corruption of hospitality. It is the condition that allows a stranger to be received at all.

  4. (D)evidence that strangers were usually welcomed without any suspicion at all.

Paragraph 2 says the rituals 'can be read as stages in this conversion', the conversion of an 'uncertain figure into a guest'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 begins by describing the stranger as a possible 'spy or a raider'; the rituals manage that uncertainty rather than showing it was absent.

Common misconception: Treating the rituals as gestures of pure kindness instead of reading the sentence that frames them.

CAT-VARC-P03-043-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The passage implies that the customary limit on the length of a guest's stay
  1. (A)reflects the small amount of food that most households could afford to share.

Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says a guest who could stay indefinitely 'would gradually unsettle the household's order, and the host would cease to be master of his own house'; paragraph 4 says the host's authority is 'the condition that allows a stranger to be received at all'. Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says the welcome 'is generous precisely because it is temporary', and paragraph 4 denies that the host's authority corrupts hospitality.

Why not (D): Adoption is listed in paragraph 3 as one of the statuses a guest may pass into after the limit, not as something the limit prevents.

CAT-VARC-P03-044 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Easy · 417 words · 4 questions

A parable that became famous in the second half of the twentieth century asks us to imagine a pasture open to all. Each herder, reasoning that the benefit of an extra animal is his alone while the cost of overgrazing is shared by everyone, adds another, and then another, until the grass is gone and all are ruined. The moral usually drawn is stark: resources held in common will be destroyed unless they are divided into private plots or placed under the control of the state.

The parable is persuasive, but it describes something that most actual commons are not. In many villages, pastures, woodlots, fishing ponds and irrigation channels have been shared for generations without being exhausted, and not because the villagers were unusually selfless. They were governed by rules. A household might be allowed to graze only as many animals as it could feed through the winter from its own fields; grazing might move between sections of the hill in a fixed rotation; a watchman, paid in grain, might patrol the boundary, and offenders might face fines that rose with each repeated offence. The herders of the parable have no such rules, no means of watching one another and no way of punishing those who cheat. What the parable describes is not a commons but open access, a resource that belongs to no community at all.

This does not mean that village commons always survive. They tend to fail in recognisable circumstances: when outsiders who are not bound by local rules can enter freely, when a distant authority overrides local arrangements with regulations that villagers neither understand nor respect, when migration thins the community so much that no one is left to keep watch, or when a sudden rise in the value of the resource makes cheating more tempting than it has ever been. These are real dangers. But they are dangers to particular institutions, not proof that shared ownership is doomed by human nature.

The practical lesson is that the familiar choice between private property and state control is too narrow. To say so is not to romanticise village life; such arrangements can be strict, and some are unequal, giving larger shares to households that hold more land. Even so, a third possibility, communities managing their own resources by rules they have made and can enforce, has a long record of success, and policy that ignores it may destroy working arrangements in the name of preventing a tragedy that was never going to happen.

CAT-VARC-P03-045 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Difficult · 495 words · 4 questions

For a long time the dominant attitude of academic history towards oral tradition was one of polite suspicion. A story passed from mouth to mouth over many generations, it was argued, must accumulate errors as surely as a manuscript copied by careless scribes, and in the absence of any original against which to check it, the historian had no way of telling the accurate parts from the embellishments. Against this, a more recent and more sympathetic current has insisted that societies without writing developed techniques of memorisation of extraordinary precision, and that their traditions deserve to be treated as archives in their own right. Each side, I want to suggest, misconceives the object it is arguing about.

The error both share is to assume that an oral tradition is trying to do what a written chronicle does, namely preserve a record of past events for its own sake, and to differ only on how well it succeeds. Traditions are rarely maintained for that purpose. A genealogy recited at the installation of a chief exists to establish that he is entitled to the office; an account of the first settlers exists to explain why one lineage holds the best land and another serves as its ritual guardians. Because the tradition answers to present needs, it changes when those needs change, and its changes are not random. Lines that no longer matter drop out; ancestors who connect currently allied groups acquire importance; the long, uneventful middle of the past is compressed, so that the founders seem only a few generations removed from those who remember their grandparents.

This is precisely why the sceptic's analogy with the copied manuscript is misleading. Random copying errors cannot be corrected without an original, but distortions that follow from a known function can, at least in part, be anticipated and read through. A historian who understands that a genealogy is a charter for office will be cautious about its middle generations and its convenient links, while attending closely to details that serve no present interest and are therefore unlikely to have been invented. Nor is all content equally fluid: formulaic songs, names of places and fixed ritual phrases are often transmitted with a fidelity that ordinary narrative never achieves, even when no one any longer understands what they mean.

None of this licenses the opposite romanticism. A tradition that is reliable about the shape of an ancient migration may be quite wrong about who led it, and no technique of memorisation can protect a story from a community's interest in retelling it. Written sources, it should be added, are hardly exempt from the same pressures; charters and chronicles were also composed to justify claims, and historians learned long ago to read them against the grain. Oral tradition, approached in that spirit, is neither an archive nor a fiction. It is a record of what a community needed its past to be, and that is itself a kind of evidence about the past.

  2. (B)Accounts of who led an ancient migration, which communities have reason to remember exactly.
  3. (C)Fixed forms such as formulaic songs and ritual phrases, even when their meaning has been lost.
  4. (D)Stories that explain why one lineage holds the best land in the settlement.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says 'formulaic songs, names of places and fixed ritual phrases are often transmitted with a fidelity that ordinary narrative never achieves, even when no one any longer understands what they mean.'

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the middle of the past is 'compressed', and paragraph 3 advises caution about 'its middle generations'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says a tradition 'may be quite wrong about who led it', the opposite of fidelity.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 gives these as stories shaped by present needs, which change 'when those needs change'.

Common misconception: Confusing 'details that serve no present interest', which are judged trustworthy, with the compressed middle generations.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in the last sentence of paragraph 3. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-046 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Medium · 430 words · 4 questions

It has become common for conservation projects and agricultural agencies to announce that they have 'validated' some piece of local knowledge. Fishers' accounts of where a species gathers to spawn are checked against tagging data; herders' claims about which plants their animals avoid in a dry year are tested in feeding trials; farmers' weather signs are compared with rainfall records. When the local claim survives, it is welcomed into the scientific literature, and the project is praised for taking seriously people whose expertise was long ignored. This is an improvement on simple dismissal. Yet the language of validation carries assumptions that deserve more scrutiny than they usually receive.

The first is that local knowledge is a set of separate statements, each of which can be lifted out and checked on its own. Much of what farmers and fishers know does not take this form. A farmer who decides when to sow may attend at once to the flowering of a particular tree, the behaviour of ants, the smell of the soil and the memory of the last three seasons, weighing them against one another in ways she could not easily put into words. Test any one of these signs in isolation and it may predict rainfall poorly; to conclude that the farmer's knowledge has failed would be to mistake a judgement for a list of rules. What is being evaluated is not what she knows but a fragment translated into a form the evaluator can handle.

The second assumption is that the direction of authority runs one way. Science sets the standard, local knowledge applies for admission, and once admitted it belongs to the literature rather than to the people who held it. Communities have sometimes found that knowledge about medicinal plants or fishing grounds, once published, was used by outsiders in ways they had no say in. Validation, in other words, can be a means of transfer as much as of recognition.

It would be wrong to conclude that local knowledge should be shielded from testing altogether. Some local beliefs are mistaken, as some scientific ones are, and the communities that hold them revise them in the light of experience more often than romantic accounts allow. The objection is not to checking but to a procedure in which only one side checks. Where farmers help decide which questions are asked and how the results will be used, and where scientific claims are also tested against their observations, the two bodies of knowledge can correct each other. That is a more demanding arrangement than validation, and a more honest one.

CAT-VARC-P03-047 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Medium · 588 words · 5 questions

One of the first lessons of economics is that money is fungible. A rupee earned is the same as a rupee found, a rupee received as a gift or a rupee won at cards; since each can buy exactly what the others can, a rational person should be indifferent to where a sum came from and should spend it according to a single ranking of needs. This is what makes money so useful. It strips goods of their particular histories and renders them comparable, and it lets a stranger in one town trade with a stranger in another without either needing to know anything about the other's life.

Ordinary households, however, behave as though they had never heard the lesson. Coins for the temple are kept in a separate tin; a grandmother's gift to a newborn goes into a savings account that no one will touch for rent even in a hard month; the money a woman earns from tailoring is spent on her children's schooling, while her husband's wages go to food and the loan; a windfall is spent on a celebration that the same sum, arriving as salary, would never have funded. Money that arrives in one way is marked, set aside and spent in another, as though each sum carried a label that its owners were bound to respect. Nor are such habits confined to poor households; in one form or another they appear in families at every level of income.

The standard response is to call this mental accounting and to treat it as a lapse, a cognitive shortcut that serves people tolerably on the whole but leads them into avoidable errors. The errors are real. A family that keeps savings in a low-interest account reserved for a daughter's wedding while borrowing at a ruinous rate to repair the roof is paying heavily for a distinction that, in purely financial terms, does not exist. Yet to describe earmarking only as a mistake is to miss what it accomplishes. The grandmother's gift is set apart because it is a relationship as much as a sum: spending it on rent would feel like dissolving the bond it expressed. The tailor's earnings are spent on schooling because keeping them distinct allows her to claim, within the household, a sphere of decision that pooled money would not give her. Labels on money are the means by which people make it say something about who they are to one another.

It may be objected that this shows only that people attach feelings to money, not that money itself ceases to be fungible. In the market, after all, the tailor's rupee buys exactly what her husband's does, and no shopkeeper asks about its origin. The objection is correct about the shopkeeper. But it concedes the point that matters. Fungibility holds where people have agreed to ignore the histories of sums, as in impersonal trade, and fails where they have not, as in the household, the family ceremony and the circle of friends who lend to one another.

The single, featureless money of the textbook, then, is not a natural property of currency that social life afterwards distorts. It is an arrangement that holds in certain settings and that people are constantly unmaking elsewhere. To see this is not to deny that the arrangement is valuable, or that families sometimes pay dearly for their labels; it is to recognise that the indifference to origins which economics takes as its starting point is itself a social achievement, and a local one.

CAT-VARC-P03-048 · Exchange and reciprocity · passage Difficult · 619 words · 5 questions

In many parts of the world, groups of neighbours, co-workers or traders meet at regular intervals and each contributes a fixed sum to a common pot, which is then handed in its entirety to one member. At the next meeting the process is repeated and the pot goes to someone else, until every member has received it once and the cycle ends or begins again. The order may be fixed by lot, by agreement or, in some versions, by bidding, with the member who most urgently wants the money accepting a smaller pot and the difference shared among the rest. Such rotating associations go by dozens of local names, and for a long time development economists regarded them as makeshift substitutes for banks, ingenious but destined to disappear once formal credit reached the people who used them.

That prediction has fared poorly. The associations persist among people who have bank accounts, and they have travelled with migrants into cities where banks are on every corner. Their persistence is puzzling on a narrow financial view. For those who receive the pot early, the arrangement is an interest-free loan; for those who receive it late, it is a savings scheme that pays no interest at all, and that exposes them to the risk that an early recipient will stop contributing once he has been paid. A late member could, it seems, do better by saving the same sums in a bank, and an early member would be the obvious candidate for default.

The usual solutions to the puzzle point to discipline. Saving alone requires a resolve that many people find hard to sustain; a contribution owed to a group that meets every month, whose members will notice and remember a missed payment, supplies that resolve from outside. This is surely part of the answer. But it treats the problem as one between the saver and her own weaker self, whereas members themselves often describe it differently. What makes saving hard, they say, is not temptation so much as other people: the brother who needs money for a wedding, the cousin whose shop has failed, the neighbour whose child is ill. A sum in a bank account is visible to such claims and difficult to refuse. A sum owed to the association is not available, and saying so is an excuse that kin and neighbours recognise as legitimate.

On this reading the association is not a crude version of a bank but an instrument for managing obligations, one that uses a debt to the group to shelter money from other debts that are harder to decline. The reading also explains why the associations depend so heavily on the density of the ties among their members. The same social pressure that makes a refusal to a cousin acceptable is what keeps the early recipient paying; where members can move away without consequence, or barely know one another, the associations break down, and stories of a member who vanished with the pot are common enough to show that the risk is not imaginary.

None of this implies that formal finance has nothing to offer the people who join such groups, or that the associations are always wise; members of bidding versions sometimes accept heavy discounts out of desperation, and the losses from a collapse can be severe. The point is narrower and, I think, more instructive. The rotating association survives not because its members lack access to something better but because it does something that an ordinary bank account, by design, does not: it makes a sum of money socially unavailable. The question its persistence raises is less why the poor lack banks than what banks, as they are usually designed, lack.

  3. (C)the order in which members receive the pot is fixed by drawing lots at the first meeting.
  4. (D)members compete to receive the pot as late as possible in order to earn the most interest.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 1 says that in bidding versions 'the member who most urgently wants the money' accepts 'a smaller pot and the difference shared among the rest'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says every member contributes 'a fixed sum', so no member contributes more than another.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 names lot as a separate method of fixing the order, distinct from bidding.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says a late recipient's savings pay 'no interest at all'; bidding is for early access by the one who 'most urgently wants the money'.

Common misconception: Confusing bidding with the other ways of fixing the order listed in the same sentence.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one clause of paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-049 · Institutions and trust · passage Easy · 461 words · 4 questions

It is often said that the orderly queue reveals something about a people's character, as if patience were a national trait passed down with the language. The explanation is flattering to those who queue well and insulting to those who do not, and it is mostly wrong. A queue is better understood as a small institution: a shared rule, first come first served, enforced by no official and sustained by people who mostly do not know one another and will never meet again. What needs explaining, therefore, is not why some nations are supposedly virtuous but why such a rule, lacking any formal sanction, holds at all among strangers.

Part of the answer is that the queue makes its own rule visible. Everyone can see the line, everyone can see their place in it, and anyone who pushes in is caught in the act before an audience that has every reason to object. The penalty is rarely more than a sharp word or a collective stare, yet it is usually enough, because the offender cannot pretend not to know the rule. Compare a crowd waiting at the doors of a bus, where no line has formed. Here the same people who would wait patiently at a ticket counter will often jostle, not because they have become selfish but because no one can say with confidence who arrived first. Without a visible order there is nothing to defend and no clear offence to resent.

This suggests that the strength of an informal norm depends less on the virtue of those who follow it than on how legible it makes compliance and violation. Managers of busy offices seem to have grasped this when they introduced numbered tickets: the ticket does not change anyone's character, but it turns an ambiguous crowd into an order that each person can check. Railings, painted footprints and single winding lines that feed many counters work in the same way. The same logic explains why a queue that winds around a corner, so that newcomers cannot tell where it ends, tends to generate precisely the disputes that a visible line prevents. Such devices are inexpensive, and they succeed by removing doubt rather than by threatening punishment.

None of this means that the queue is the fairest possible way to allocate a scarce service. It rewards those with time to spare and ignores urgency; a hospital that treated patients strictly in order of arrival would be orderly and indefensible. Nor should the absence of lines be read as disorder, since a crowd may follow subtler conventions that an outsider fails to notice. The point is narrower. Where people do accept first come first served, the rule survives among strangers because it can be seen, and it falters where it cannot.

Paragraph 1 rejects the character explanation and asks why the rule 'holds at all among strangers'; paragraphs 2 and 3 answer that the queue 'makes its own rule visible'; paragraph 4 restates that the rule 'survives among strangers because it can be seen'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 calls the national character explanation 'mostly wrong'; this presents the rejected view as the author's purpose.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 mentions tickets and railings as illustrations of legibility; the passage never ranks them as the most effective means, and they are not its purpose.

Common misconception: Picking the national character view because the passage opens with it, or an illustration from paragraph 3.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated in paragraph 1 and restated in the last sentence; the rejected view is flagged as wrong. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-050 · Institutions and trust · passage Easy · 425 words · 4 questions

Over the past few decades many voluntary organisations have come to manage their volunteers much as firms manage staff. There are role descriptions and application forms, induction sessions, supervisors and annual counts of hours given. The change is usually presented as simple progress. Volunteers who know what is expected of them are said to stay longer, and organisations that can report their hours to funders are better placed to win support. Funders, understandably, prefer measurable outputs, and organisations competing for limited grants have every incentive to supply them. Both claims are plausible, and neither is the whole story.

What the managerial model tends to forget is that volunteering was never only a cheaper form of labour. People who give their time without pay are also choosing what to do, with whom and on what terms, and that choice is part of what they value. A retired teacher who helped run a reading club because she liked the children and the other helpers may find, once the club has become a programme with targets and a coordinator, that she has been turned into an unpaid employee of something she no longer recognises. She has lost not her wages, which she never had, but her say. Many such volunteers do not complain; they simply drift away, and their departure is recorded, if at all, as a problem of retention.

There is also a quieter loss. Informal associations of volunteers have long been places where people who would never meet at work learn to cooperate, argue and decide things together. A committee that argues for an entire evening over the date of a jumble sale is, among other things, rehearsing the arts of compromise and accountability on which larger institutions depend. When decisions are taken by paid staff and volunteers are slotted into predefined roles, that practice of self-government shrinks. The organisation may deliver its service more reliably while doing less to cultivate the habits that made voluntary life worth defending.

None of this is an argument against training or rules as such. A rescue team on a mountain cannot depend on whoever turns up with good intentions, and anyone who works with children ought to be checked. The difficulty arises when standards suited to such demanding tasks are applied across the board, to the allotment society and the neighbourhood library as much as to the lifeboat crew. The sensible question is not whether to professionalise but where, and the answer should depend on what the task requires rather than on what funders find easy to count.

  3. (C)retired teachers are especially ill suited to working under coordinators because they are used to running their own classrooms.
  4. (D)the reading club ceased to be of any benefit to the children once it was turned into a programme with targets.

CAT-VARC-P03-050-Q3 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the role of the third paragraph in the passage?

CAT-VARC-P03-051 · Institutions and trust · passage Medium · 434 words · 4 questions

Few school practices seem as commonsensical as grouping pupils by ability. A teacher facing a class whose members range from those who can barely read to those who devour novels must, it seems, either bore the strong or lose the weak, and sorting children into streams promises to spare her that dilemma. Each group can be taught at its own pace, with material pitched to its level; nobody is held back and nobody is left behind. The argument has the appeal of efficiency, and in a narrow sense it is often right.

The trouble lies in what a stream does after it has been formed. Placement usually happens early and rests on tests that measure, along with ability, a child's maturity, her familiarity with the kind of task being set and the amount of help she has had at home. Children who are young for their year are a familiar case: a few months' difference that would be invisible at fifteen can look like a difference of talent at seven. Once placed, however, pupils do not simply learn at different speeds. The lower streams are often given a thinner curriculum, less experienced teachers and quieter expectations, while the upper streams are prepared for examinations the others will never be entered for. The gap that the original test detected is thus widened by the arrangement that was supposed to respond to it, and the later results, which seem to confirm the placement, are partly its product.

Defenders of streaming reply that pupils can move between groups, and in principle they can. In practice movement is uncommon, and it is not difficult to see why. A child in a lower stream who has covered less material cannot readily join a group that has covered more, so even a genuine improvement in her understanding may never show up as a change of placement. Teachers, moreover, come to know their pupils as members of a particular set, and the label shapes how her work is read. The door is open, but the corridor behind it grows longer every term.

It would be a mistake to conclude that all grouping is harmful. Teaching a class of widely mixed attainment is genuinely demanding, and some arrangements avoid the worst effects of streaming: grouping pupils separately for particular subjects, regrouping them frequently and keeping the curriculum common, so that a move between groups remains a realistic possibility rather than a formal one. What distinguishes these arrangements from the rigid stream is not that they ignore differences in attainment but that they refuse to treat a single early measurement as a verdict.

  3. (C)chiefly the quality of the teaching that each child has received in the school before the tests.
  4. (D)a child's real talent more accurately at the age of seven than it could be measured at fifteen.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 states that placement 'rests on tests that measure, along with ability, a child's maturity, her familiarity with the kind of task being set and the amount of help she has had at home'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the tests measure 'along with ability' maturity, familiarity with the task and help at home, so ability alone is wrong.

Why not (C): The passage names help 'at home', maturity and familiarity with the task, not the quality of earlier school teaching.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 implies the reverse: a small age gap that 'would be invisible at fifteen can look like a difference of talent at seven'.

Common misconception: Overlooking the phrase 'along with ability' and treating the tests as pure measures.

Difficulty: The answer is a close paraphrase of one sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-052 · Institutions and trust · passage Medium · 418 words · 4 questions

When a welfare benefit goes unclaimed by many of the people entitled to it, the usual explanation is that they did not know about it, did not need it badly enough or could not be bothered. The explanation locates the problem in the claimant. It rarely asks what claiming actually involves: discovering that the benefit exists, working out whether one qualifies, gathering documents that may be held by three different offices, completing a form whose questions assume a settled address and a regular income, and then, often, doing it all again a few months later to prove that nothing has changed.

These demands are usually described as administrative friction, an unfortunate but neutral by-product of running any large programme. They are not neutral. Every hour required to claim is an hour that some people can spare more easily than others, and the people least able to spare it are frequently those the programme was meant to help: the seriously ill, those working irregular shifts, those for whom scarcity has already consumed much of the attention that form-filling demands. A requirement that costs a comfortable applicant an afternoon may cost a struggling one the benefit itself.

There is a further reason to look closely at such burdens. Because they are set through forms, procedures and office hours rather than through legislation, they can change who receives a benefit without any public decision that it should. A government that wished to spend less on a programme but did not wish to say so could add a document to the list or shorten the period for renewal, and the number of recipients would fall while the rules of entitlement stayed exactly as they were. Whether or not anyone intends this, the effect is a form of policy made out of sight, insulated from the debate that a change in the law would provoke.

This is not to say that verification is illegitimate. Public programmes depend on the belief that money goes to those entitled to it, and some checking sustains that belief among the taxpayers who fund them. But the costs of checking are borne in two places, in the office and in the lives of applicants, and only the first tends to be counted. A sound design would weigh the time and strain imposed on claimants as seriously as the cost of the errors it prevents, and would treat a large gap between entitlement and receipt as evidence about the system rather than about the people outside it.

CAT-VARC-P03-053 · Institutions and trust · passage Medium · 432 words · 4 questions

Discussions of social capital commonly distinguish two kinds of tie. Bonding ties link people who are alike in some respect that matters to them, such as kinship, faith, neighbourhood or trade, and they supply loyalty and mutual help. Bridging ties link people across such lines, and they supply information, opportunities and the habit of dealing with strangers. Having drawn the distinction, many writers proceed to rank the two. Bridging is cast as the civic virtue that open societies need; bonding is regarded with suspicion, as the stuff of cliques, favouritism and inward-looking communities. The ranking is attractive partly because it flatters an ideal of openness, and partly because the dangers of tight-knit groups are so easy to picture, whereas their quieter contributions rarely make news.

The ranking is too quick. Consider a family that moves to a city where it knows no one except a handful of people from its home district. Those people find it a room, lend it money, explain which offices to visit and which employers pay on time. They are bonding ties in the purest sense, and without them the family might never acquire the stability from which wider contacts become possible. The first job outside the community, or the first friendship with a neighbour of a different background, frequently comes through someone who was met by way of the close circle. In such cases bonding is not the rival of bridging but its precondition.

It does not follow that close groups are always benign. The same density of ties that allows a community to support its members allows it to watch them, and the help it offers can come with a demand for conformity: marry within, trade within, think as we do. A young woman who wishes to train for an occupation the group disapproves of may find that the network which once sustained her family now stands in her way, and that the price of leaving it is the loss of everything it provided. Here the closeness that was a resource becomes a closure.

What separates the two cases is not the strength of the bonding ties but their permeability: whether membership of the close group is compatible with forming ties outside it, or is made conditional on refusing them. A policy that tried to weaken community networks in the name of integration would therefore risk destroying the very support that allows newcomers to reach beyond those networks in the first place. A better question to ask of any close group is not how tightly it binds but whether it lets its members go.

Common misconception: Importing the conformity demands of paragraph 3 into the family example of paragraph 2.

CAT-VARC-P03-054 · Institutions and trust · passage Medium · 435 words · 4 questions

Introducing video review into professional sport seemed to settle an old grievance. For as long as games have had officials, players and spectators have complained that decisions were wrong, and now there was a technology that could show, from several angles and at any speed, what had really happened. Few people expected that the result would be a new and more corrosive kind of dispute. Yet that is what many supporters now describe: arguments that once ended with the whistle continue through slow-motion replays, frozen frames and lines drawn on screens, and they seem less settled than before.

The puzzle dissolves once we ask what the referee's authority was for. It was never simply a matter of being right. A match needs some means of converting a contested event into a settled fact quickly enough for play to continue, and the referee supplied it by being final. Players accepted decisions they believed mistaken not because they trusted the official's eyesight but because they had accepted, in joining the game, that someone must decide and that the decision would stand. Error was tolerated as the price of finality, and since the errors fell, over a season, on all sides alike, the price seemed bearable.

Review alters this bargain in two ways. First, it relocates authority. The decision now seems to belong to the replay, and the referee on the field becomes a provisional judge whose rulings await confirmation from elsewhere. Second, and less obviously, it changes the kind of question being asked. Whether a challenge was reckless, or whether an attacker gained an advantage, is a matter of judgement that a trained official can make in a moment. Whether a toe was ahead of a defender's shoulder at the instant the ball was struck is presented as a matter of fact, yet the answer depends on which frame is chosen and where the line is drawn, choices that the apparent precision of the image conceals. A decision dressed as measurement invites challenge in a way that an acknowledged judgement does not, because every measurement can be disputed by another.

None of this is a case for abandoning the cameras. A goal awarded for a shot that plainly missed, or a player sent off for a foul committed by someone else, is an injustice that no appeal to finality can make acceptable, and replays remove such errors cheaply. The mistake lies in treating review as a means of perfecting every marginal call. Confined to clear and obvious errors, it supports the referee's authority; extended to the margins, it dissolves the very finality that made officiating possible.

CAT-VARC-P03-054-Q2 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The contrast in paragraph 3 between whether 'a challenge was reckless' and whether 'a toe was ahead of a defender's shoulder' serves to
  1. (A)illustrate the claim that officials on the field judge fouls less competently than the officials who watch replays.
  2. (B)argue that video review ought to be restricted to questions that can be settled as matters of measurable fact.
  3. (C)show that offside decisions are easier for officials to get right than decisions about how reckless a challenge was.
  4. (D)distinguish a question openly treated as judgement from one presented as fact that in truth rests on concealed choices.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 calls the first 'a matter of judgement' and says the second 'is presented as a matter of fact, yet the answer depends on which frame is chosen and where the line is drawn'. The contrast sets up the claim that 'A decision dressed as measurement invites challenge'.

Why not (A): The passage says a trained official can judge recklessness 'in a moment'; it makes no claim about relative competence.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 restricts review to 'clear and obvious errors'; the author doubts that marginal measurement questions are settled facts at all.

Why not (C): The author says the toe question depends on hidden choices of frame and line, which makes it harder to settle, not easier.

Common misconception: Assuming the author favours review for measurable facts, when the passage questions whether marginal measurements are really facts.

Difficulty: The function depends on the second of two changes the paragraph describes, which is introduced as 'less obvious'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-054-Q3 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely agree with which of the following?
  1. (A)The disputes that review provokes show that replays are less accurate than the eyes of a trained referee.
  2. (B)A replay system can remove real injustices and still weaken officiating if it is used to settle marginal calls.
  3. (C)Review would provoke no new disputes if the lines drawn on screens were made more precise than they are now.
  4. (D)Since finality matters more than accuracy in sport, cameras used for review do more harm to games than good.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says replays remove plain errors such as a goal for a shot that 'plainly missed', but 'extended to the margins, it dissolves the very finality that made officiating possible'. Both halves of the correct answer come from that paragraph.

Why not (A): The author's complaint concerns finality and concealed choices of frame and line, not the accuracy of replays compared with referees' eyes.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says the 'apparent precision of the image conceals' choices; greater precision would leave the choice of frame open, and 'every measurement can be disputed by another'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 opens 'None of this is a case for abandoning the cameras' and says replays remove blatant errors 'cheaply'.

Common misconception: Choosing the option against cameras because the passage stresses finality over accuracy, missing the concession; or the precision option, missing the point about frames.

CAT-VARC-P03-054-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's explanation of why disputes over decisions now seem less settled?
  1. (A)In competitions that use review only for clear and obvious errors, supporters argue over decisions just as long as in those that review marginal calls.
  2. (B)Before video review was introduced, supporters often claimed that referees were biased against the team they followed.
  3. (C)Referees who work with video review report feeling less confident when making decisions during play on the field.

The author attributes the new disputes to review being 'extended to the margins', where decisions are 'dressed as measurement'. If competitions that avoid marginal review have equally long disputes, that explanation loses its force.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 already grants that complaints are as old as officials; past accusations do not bear on why today's disputes seem less settled.

Why not (C): This fits the author's claim that review 'relocates authority' and makes the referee 'a provisional judge', so it supports rather than weakens.
