CAT-VARC-P03-055 · Institutions and trust · passage Difficult · 590 words · 5 questions

Every few years a new survey announces that trust in public institutions has fallen again. Respondents are asked how much confidence they have in parliament, the courts, the police or the press, their answers are averaged, and the declining line is offered as evidence of a crisis. The finding is treated as self-interpreting: less trust is bad, more trust is good, and the task of leaders is to restore it. Each step of that reasoning deserves more scrutiny than it usually receives.

Begin with what the question measures. A person asked how far she trusts the courts may be reporting her estimate of their competence, her view of their honesty, her approval of recent verdicts, or simply her mood about public life in general. Answers about quite different institutions tend to rise and fall together, which suggests that much of what is captured is a general disposition rather than a considered judgement about any particular body. Nor does the answer reliably predict conduct. People who tell interviewers that they distrust the courts continue to sign contracts that only courts can enforce, and people who profess little confidence in the health service continue to take the medicines it prescribes. If trust is fundamentally a matter of what we are prepared to rely on, the survey and the behaviour can come apart, and it is not obvious that the survey is the better witness.

A second difficulty is that falling trust is ambiguous even where it is real. It may mean that institutions have become less reliable. It may also mean that citizens have come to expect more of them, or that information about their failures, once hidden, now circulates freely. In the last case the decline records not a deterioration in the institution but an improvement in the public's knowledge of it. The philosophical point beneath this is simple and often forgotten: trust is valuable only when it is placed in what is trustworthy. A population that trusted a corrupt police force completely would not be enjoying a civic good; it would be making a mistake, and one that the corrupt would be glad to exploit. What we should want is not high trust but well-calibrated trust, trust that rises and falls with the reliability of its object.

This does not license complacency. Distrust, warranted or not, has consequences of its own. Citizens who doubt that taxes are spent honestly may be readier to evade them; communities that expect the police to be indifferent may stop reporting crimes, which in turn makes the police less effective and the distrust more justified. A decline in measured trust can therefore be the beginning of a spiral that no recalibration will halt by itself, because the institution's reliability depends partly on the cooperation that distrust withdraws. The point is not that the numbers are meaningless but that they cannot, on their own, tell us whether we face a failing institution, a better-informed public or a passing mood.

What would tell us more is evidence of a different kind: whether expressions of trust track independent measures of institutional performance over time, whether people who say they distrust an institution still rely on it when something important is at stake, and whether trust differs between those who have dealt with an institution directly and those who know it only by reputation. Such evidence is harder to gather than a single question repeated every few years, and it yields no simple line on a chart for commentators to lament. That is precisely its value.

CAT-VARC-P03-055-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, the tendency of answers about quite different institutions to rise and fall together suggests that
  1. (A)the various institutions have tended to improve or to decline in their performance at the same times.
  2. (B)survey responses largely reflect a general disposition rather than considered judgements about particular bodies.
  3. (C)people trust the courts and the press for exactly the same reasons as each other.
  4. (D)respondents are much better informed about some public institutions than they are about others.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: answers about different institutions 'tend to rise and fall together, which suggests that much of what is captured is a general disposition rather than a considered judgement about any particular body'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 draws the opposite conclusion: the pattern suggests the answers capture 'a general disposition' rather than each body's performance.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 lists several possible reasons for any answer and does not claim that the reasons for trusting different bodies are identical.

Why not (D): Differences in how well informed respondents are play no part in paragraph 2's interpretation of the shared movement.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about shared performance, an explanation the author does not adopt.

Difficulty: Located in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-055-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
As used in paragraph 3, 'well-calibrated trust' refers to trust that
  1. (A)stays at a moderate level, avoiding both complete confidence and complete suspicion of an institution.
  2. (B)is based on direct personal dealings with an institution rather than on its reputation alone.
  3. (C)rises or falls according to how reliable the institution in which it is placed actually is.
  4. (D)remains steady over the years regardless of how an institution's record happens to change.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 explains the phrase directly: 'trust that rises and falls with the reliability of its object', following the claim that 'trust is valuable only when it is placed in what is trustworthy'.

Why not (A): Calibration is defined as trust 'that rises and falls with the reliability of its object'; it can be high where an institution is highly reliable, so moderation is not the point.

Why not (B): Direct dealings appear in paragraph 5 as one kind of evidence to gather; they are not what paragraph 3 means by calibration.

Why not (D): This is the opposite of calibration, which requires trust to change as reliability changes.

Common misconception: Equating calibration with moderation, a natural reading of 'well-calibrated' that the passage's definition does not support.

Difficulty: The moderate-level option is a plausible everyday sense of the phrase; the passage defines it differently. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-055-Q3 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P03-055-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ

  2. (B)People who had dealt with the courts directly reported higher trust in them than people who knew the courts only by their reputation among friends and neighbours.

Why not (B): This bears on paragraph 5's point about direct experience, not on whether the fall reflects better information.

Why not (C): Wider circulation of information is what the explanation predicts, so this supports it rather than weakening it.

CAT-VARC-P03-055-Q5 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author would most likely agree with which of the following?

CAT-VARC-P03-056 · Institutions and trust · passage Difficult · 575 words · 5 questions

A familiar story about civic life holds that ordinary people have withdrawn from it. Where their grandparents joined lodges, unions, parents' associations and choral societies, attended meetings and stood for office in them, people today are said to stay at home, absorbed in private amusements and too busy or too distrustful to join anything. The story is usually told as a matter of individual choice and individual failing, and the remedies proposed follow naturally from it: people should be encouraged, shamed or gently nudged back into joining.

There is another way to read much of the same evidence. What has declined is not association as such but a particular form of it: the federation of local branches, each with its own officers, meetings and dues, linked through elected delegates to regional and national bodies. In its place, many of the organisations that now claim to speak for citizens are run by professional staff from a central office. They recruit supporters through mailing lists and advertisements, ask them for money rather than time, and consult them, if at all, through surveys. Their membership lists may be long, but a member is someone who has made a payment, not someone who has attended a meeting, and nothing in the organisation's routine requires that members ever meet one another.

Read this way, the decline is less a withdrawal from below than a transformation from above. Organisations discovered that it was cheaper and more effective, at least for the purposes of lobbying and publicity, to raise money from many distant supporters than to sustain an expensive structure of local branches with their own halls, officers and opinions. Funders, too, preferred bodies with a clear national voice and a small, accountable staff, and the habits of professional management spread from one organisation to the next. The people who drifted away from the older associations may not have rejected participation at all; they may simply have been offered fewer occasions for it.

The distinction matters because the federated association did something its successors do not. It taught people with no political ambitions how to run a meeting, keep minutes, manage a budget, argue for a motion and accept defeat when it was voted down. These were not trivial accomplishments; they were the ordinary apprenticeship of democratic citizenship for many who would never hold public office. It also tied the local to the national: a member in a small town could, through a chain of elected representatives, influence what the national body did. A donor to a professionally managed organisation may support its aims with real conviction, but she learns none of these skills and holds no comparable thread of influence over what is done in her name.

It would be easy to turn this into nostalgia, and nostalgia would mislead. Many of the old associations excluded women or people of the wrong background, and their local meetings could be stifling and petty. The new organisations, for their part, have often been more effective at changing laws. Nor have people stopped gathering; they form online groups and informal networks with remarkable ease. But these are seldom built to govern themselves through elected officers and binding votes. If the loss is real, it is the loss of a school of self-government, and it will not be repaired by urging individuals to join more. It would require organisations to find reasons, and funders to give them reasons, to want members who turn up.

CAT-VARC-P03-056-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, in the professionally managed organisations a member is typically
  1. (A)a person whose views on the organisation's policy are regularly sought and acted upon through surveys.
  2. (B)a person who gives time at the central office, working alongside the organisation's professional staff.
  3. (C)a person chosen by a local branch to represent it at the meetings of a regional or national body.
  4. (D)a person whose membership consists in having contributed money rather than in taking part in meetings.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2: 'a member is someone who has made a payment, not someone who has attended a meeting'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says supporters are consulted through surveys 'if at all', and says nothing of their views being acted upon.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says these organisations 'ask them for money rather than time'.

Why not (C): Elected delegates belong to the older federations described at the start of paragraph 2, not to the staff-run organisations.

Common misconception: Confusing the features of the federations with those of their successors, both described in paragraph 2.

Difficulty: Answer is in one sentence; the wrong options draw on nearby sentences. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P03-056-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author's reading of the evidence implies that the remedies mentioned at the end of paragraph 1 are
  1. (A)unnecessary, since people already gather in online groups and networks that serve the same civic purposes.
  2. (B)suitable for older citizens but not for younger people who grew up absorbed in private amusements.
  3. (C)misdirected, since they target individuals' willingness to join when the change lay largely in what organisations offered.
  4. (D)sound in their aim but too gentle, since only shaming would be strong enough to bring people back to meetings.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 calls the decline 'less a withdrawal from below than a transformation from above', and paragraph 5 says it 'will not be repaired by urging individuals to join more'. Remedies aimed at individuals miss the organisational cause.

Why not (B): The passage makes no distinction between age groups; private amusements belong to the familiar story the author questions.

Why not (D): The author says the loss 'will not be repaired by urging individuals to join more', rejecting the approach, not its strength.

Common misconception: Choosing the online groups option because paragraph 5 acknowledges that people still gather.

CAT-VARC-P03-056-Q4 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the central claim of the passage?
  1. (A)The fall in civic membership owes more to how organisations were rebuilt than to individual withdrawal, and it has cost citizens practice in governing themselves.
  2. (B)The decline of membership associations reflects organisations' choices about cheaper ways of raising money rather than any change in what ordinary people want.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 says the decline is 'a transformation from above', paragraph 4 says the federations taught skills of governance their successors do not, and paragraph 5 names the loss as 'a school of self-government'. The correct answer combines the cause and the cost.

Why not (B): This takes paragraph 3's reinterpretation but hardens its hedge ('may not have rejected participation at all') into a flat denial, and it leaves out the cost of the change that paragraphs 4 and 5 argue for.

Why not (C): This is the 'familiar story' of paragraph 1, which the author offers 'another way to read'.

CAT-VARC-P03-056-Q5 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following would the author most likely regard as a step towards repairing the loss the passage describes?
  1. (A)A national charity doubles its number of supporters by advertising widely and begins to consult them through more frequent and detailed surveys.
  2. (B)A national charity gives local groups of its supporters budgets to manage and lets them elect delegates who vote on its policy.
  3. (C)An online forum lets supporters of a cause exchange views freely and at length, without officers, budgets or binding votes.
  4. (D)A public campaign urges every citizen to join at least one association in their town and to attend its meetings.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The loss is of practice in running meetings, managing budgets and voting, and of a chain of elected representation from local to national (paragraph 4). A charity that gives local groups budgets and elected delegates restores both, and paragraph 5 asks organisations to 'want members who turn up'.

Why not (A): This deepens the model of paragraph 2, where supporters give money and are consulted 'through surveys', without meetings or elected officers.

Why not (C): Paragraph 5 says such groups 'are seldom built to govern themselves through elected officers and binding votes', which this forum lacks.

Why not (D): Paragraph 5 says the loss 'will not be repaired by urging individuals to join more'; this is the individual remedy the author rejects.

Common misconception: Choosing the public campaign because it encourages attendance, without noticing that the author rejects remedies aimed at individuals.

Difficulty: The campaign option sounds like participation; rejecting it needs the author's diagnosis that the cause lies with organisations. · Exams: CAT, XAT
